Cities: Skylines' Best Replacement Is Finally Here and Better than Expected

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Transport Fever 3 turned me into a logistics nerd

Transport Fever 3 turned me into a logistics nerd

2026 is the year I got massively into city builders, and by massively I mean I played Cities: Skylines more than anything else for the first half of the year. I started dabbling with city builder-slash-logistics games in the second half of the year, like Anno 117, and turned my attention to Transport Fever 3 soon after. When I first learned about Transport Fever 3, I called it "like" Cities: Skylines. It looked a lot like a traditional city builder, albeit one where you focus on roads more than zoning. After playing it over the last month or so, though, I realize the difference between city builder and logistics planner is a lot more distinct than I initially gave it credit for — and I've irreversibly become a logistics sicko.

The first thing you do in Cities: Skylines is build a road, and then you start earmarking plots of land for residential and commercial zoning. The biggest logistics puzzles you end up solving involve road improvements, or more accurately, managing traffic as your city gets bigger. It's a design and aesthetics challenge more than anything, though. In the struggle to make roads big enough to handle the needs of a growing city, keeping things from looking like a dystopian hellscape is more important than optimizing traffic flow. Well, for me it is anyway, but the game's comparatively lax management requirements let personal preference prevail over logistical needs.

Not so with Transport Fever 3. Here, you start with a small town and a burgeoning transport company. The first task is creating a small network of transport lines to support consumer travel and industrial growth. Then you encourage new industry by making more lines that connect resource deposits with processing facilities in, or near, the town. This small-but-important difference captured me instantly. Planning industries in Cities usually takes little foresight. Once you have the money, you just construct the necessary buildings as close to the resource as possible and make sure there's a road that can link them back to the main city for deliveries. Most resource nodes exist on map tiles outside your main metro area anyway, so you can just position any old road how you’d like and go on about your business. Or you can just ignore industry entirely and turn your town into a strip mall paradise.

But the goal in Transport Fever 3 is to grow the existing town, and it won't grow without industry. More people need more food, more places to work, and more ways to get to work, ideally on time and without being crammed on a tiny coach ill-adapted for the purpose. You find places suitable for residential building and industry like in Cities, but it all builds up based on roads and road types, which gives you more control over how everything develops and more challenges to solve. Opting for a country road influences the kind of residential areas that will populate along it and makes it easier to create neighborhood-like areas, for example, as opposed to having homes just pop up inconveniently on a standard road. Country roads aren't suitable for all types of travel, though, so you have to find alternative solutions to ensure the people living there have access to all the goods and transportation methods they need. Multiply those considerations for every new residential and commercial area, imagine designing a functional and aesthetically pleasing infrastructure network to accommodate them, and you've got an idea of how this game works.

There are a slew of other considerations to deal with, too. Terrain gradient determines the type of road you need — and now much it'll cost to maintain — so freight vehicles don't get stuck. Trains are wonderful for moving goods and people, but require special production chains in place to operate, which puts another burden on your local infrastructure. That's true for pretty much every transport improvement. It makes one area easier, but requires a lot more planning and work to keep others from spiraling out of control.

It's all comparatively easy to maintain when settlements are smaller, and in sandbox mode, the game doesn't punish you for keeping things small. Heck, you can have a bunch of mid-sized towns connected via water transport and airports if you want. As long as your business isn't bankrupt, you're winning. But once your town grows into a city with multiple districts, managing everything is a nested stack of challenges that just gets more complex as time passes.

A weekly cheat sheet to what you’ll eventually love to play. No buying, no commitment — just saving for later.

Which, it turns out, is exactly what I want. I frequently find myself speeding up the simulation in Cities until some disaster or other requires my direct intervention. It's very passive once you get to a certain point, where improvements are additive and routine. Make a new district, zone it like the last one — or, how about this for excitement, get a bit wild and invert the zoning swatches — connect the roads, add more power and water, proceed like before. I certainly still enjoy designing a huge city and seeing the results of my planning pay off, but I miss the hands-on requirements of early city building. That hands-off feeling never disappears in Transport Fever 3. It's a never-ending puzzle that captured me and won't let me go, and I couldn't be happier about it.

Combolands: Roguelike Citybuilder has one heck of a foundation

Opinion: England international suggests Liverpool could soon have the best player in the world

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Liverpool want to return to the pinnacle of football, and for that they need the best players on the planet.

Richard Hughes was very much motivated by this philosophy. It's why he spent so lavishly as the sporting director of the club.

He wanted to bring in the best players on the market. He did that. On paper he was successful. He secured Bradley Barcola, Alexander Isak and Florian Wirtz.

Not to mention other players for whom there was a lot of competition for as well. This includes Milos Kerkez, Jeremy Jacquet, Hugo Ekitike and Giorgi Mamardashvili.

Now aside from the notable exceptions like Ekitike and Jacquet, it's true that so far the aforementioned players have not lived up to their hype.

But transfers are judged in the long-term, not the short-term.

Julian Ward is overseeing a major reshuffleHughes has left behind a bit of a disjointed squad. Ward's job is now to fix that as Liverpool's new sporting director. It's not going to be easy. But the good thing is that the spine of the team is strong. You can comfortably build a team around Isak, Wirtz, Ekitike, Jacquet and Barcola. These are players who could become superstars and who absolutely have what it takes to be world-beaters. On top of that it's important to mention how smartly Liverpool recruited at youth level. Ifeanyi Ndukwe already looks like an incredible value for money. Mor Talla Ndiaye could prove to be the same. Let's not forget about Lucca Brughmanns and Samuel Martinez either who are very highly regarded in their respective age-groups.

Hughes has left behind a bit of a disjointed squad. Ward's job is now to fix that as Liverpool's new sporting director.

It's not going to be easy. But the good thing is that the spine of the team is strong. You can comfortably build a team around Isak, Wirtz, Ekitike, Jacquet and Barcola.

These are players who could become superstars and who absolutely have what it takes to be world-beaters.

On top of that it's important to mention how smartly Liverpool recruited at youth level. Ifeanyi Ndukwe already looks like an incredible value for money.

Mor Talla Ndiaye could prove to be the same. Let's not forget about Lucca Brughmanns and Samuel Martinez either who are very highly regarded in their respective age-groups.

Now on top of this, there's already an exciting academy thronging with burgeoning talent ready to break into Andoni Iraola's first-team.

This season's revelation has been Trey Nyoni, who has been absolutely sublime.

Rio Ngumoha has also been sensational. It's easy to forget that this is still only the beginning for the 18-year-old. Quansah who has now trained with him at club and international level speaking to BBC 5 Live confirmed that he believed that eventually Liverpool could have one of the best players on the planet.

“Rio Ngumoha," Quansah replied when asked on who he believes could win the Ballon d'Or.

"Outstanding, outstanding talent. Trained with him at Liverpool a lot," he continued.

“He’s so sharp, so quick. He’s so brave at doing his one-v-ones and definitely someone who has potential to be on that level.”

By the sounds of it, in Ngumoha Liverpool could soon be welcoming the best player on the planet.

Huelva Ibero-American Film Festival: Genre Mashups, a Vibrant Brazil and an Award for the One-Off Paulina García

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

A scene setter offering takes on this year’s Huelva Ibero-American Film Festival, which highlights 2026 standouts from Latin America and Spain

Honoring Paulina García (“Gloria”), highlighting the latest from great and fast emerging Latin American directors, the 52nd Huelva Ibero-American Film Festival lifts off Nov. 13 as a film event for our times.

Bulwarked by fast-recovering Europe and Asia, global film production in 2025 edged up to 7,707 features, higher than pre-pandemic levels of 2017-19. Latin America was no exception, up 2%. Related Stories Katseye's Megan Skiendiel Fronts Rare Beauty and Warby Parker’s New Eyewear Collaboration TV's Biggest Salaries: Zendaya, Nicolas Cage, Selena Gomez and More Stars Earning Huge Paychecks

In such a context, film festivals are worth their weight in gold as quality filters, and few are more curated than the Huelva Ibero-American Film Festival. TIFF screened 293 films in Official Selection this year. Huelva’s total lineup will feature around 100 films, festival director Manuel H. Martín tells Variety.

That’s par for the course for Huelva which will keep its main section, including a main feature and short film competition, Talento Andaluz, framing event films from Andalusia, and Acento. Cine Español, a national movie panorama.

“For us, it’s fundamental to have a festival format in line with Huelva, a friendly, close city where you can walk to places,” says Martín. “We’re aiming for a varied quality lineup with premieres for Spain and a chance to catch-up on Spanish films released during the year, but we’re not interested in growing year after year in volume.”

Huelva’s main competition will, indeed, feature just 12 titles, two more out-of-competition.

Amaia Salamanca, a star of “Grand Hotel” and “Velvet” and key in the lift-off of Spanish TV as an international phenomenon, will pick up a Luz Prize at Huelva’s opening Gala, as will Patrick Criado, good in Pedro Almodóvar’s “Bitter Christmas” and sensational in 2023 TV series “Nights in Tefia.”

However tightly curated, Huelva’s lineup also allows a broader vision of which ways Latin American cinema and film industries are heading in Latin America. A few brief provisional takes as the 52nd Huelva Ibero-American Film Festival unpools in Andalusia, running through Nov. 21:

Where’s Latin America’s cinema at? Some of its most exciting movies, as in world cinema at large, is genre bending and blending. “The Riverbank” “traverses the classic erotic thriller to experiment with tropical noir, crime drama, magical realism and even a hint of whodunnit,” Variety observes, all inspired by and exploring the reality of Recife life, director Enock Carvalho notes. “The Bovine with the Curved Horns” braids myth, oral tradition, Colombia’s armed conflict and downbeat daily life. “Days Off,” Argentine Luciana Mariani’s surprising debut melds coming of-age and sci-fi beats, as Bego, 12, experiments with shifting – the move to an alternative reality – during a solar eclipse.

García’s receives this year’s City of Huelva Prize, the festival’s highest honor. It is a measure of García’s stature that in Variety’s appreciation of Sebastián Lelio, comparing Julianne Moore’s performance in “Gloria Bell,” inspired by “Gloria,” which won García a Berlin Silver Bear in 2015, Variety does not call Moore’s a better turn but just different. “Gloria” was Lelio’s and García breakout, turning her into one of Latin America’s few performers courted by U.S. auteurs, in part for her enormous range. She is “a funny and resilient force of nature” in “Gloria,” but equally she can act a gravely ailing mother in Huelva 2026 title “Julia’s Flowers,” fading, irascible, frail, seemingly light-bodied, child-like but still, at times, capable of cognizance.

No country in Latin America has broken out to international recognition more than Brazil. Last year Walter Salles’ “I’m Still Here” won an Oscar. This year, Kleber Mendonça Filho’s “The Secret Agent” scored four Academy Award nominations. Brazil has no film of such scale this year. What it does have, however, is underscored at Huelva. It has funding from its government, “Funk” supported by a classic financing combination of public sector bank BRDE, fund FSA and the Ancine national agency. Brazil also has vibrant regional and municipal film scenes. “Funk” is set in Rio, supports by RioFilme. “The Riverbank” forms part of Recife’s vibrant film scene. The consequences? Films which have the singularity and relevance of auteur cinema but high production standards.

Or it’s got there already. Sometimes the partners align in a single territory, such as “The Bovine with the Curved Horns,” produced by Colombia’s La Fortaleza Films, Congo Films, Universidad del Magdalena, Lightbox, Pablo Mondragón, De La Tierra Producciones and Soretti Films. Or co-producers stack up internationally, such as on Lucila Mariani’s “The Days Off,” produced by Argentina’s Maravillacine (“Marilyn,” “Under the Flags, the Sun”), alongside Brazil’s Persona Non Grata Pictures and Vulcana Films (“Hard Paint,” “Night Stage”), with Mexico’s Woo Films (“Pedro Páramo,” “Noise”) and Valiente Films as co-producers. Co-production these days is a sign of necessity and partners wanting to buy into exciting propositions.

Huelva’s competition lineup features seven films by men, though two of those – “Julia’s Flowers” and “Funk” – have women protagonists. Four of the 12 have queer lead characters. Add Manuela Martelli’s “The Melting” and Fernanda Tovar’s “Sad Girls” and seven of the 14 official section movies are helmed by women. Overall, of Hueva’s around 100 titles, half are directed by women, notes Martin. Four of the 14 films have LGBTQ lead characters. Huelva is, however, something of an exception when it comes to festivals and women’s films and the world. LGBTQ freedoms remains a barometer of larger freedoms in many Latin American films, such as “Riverbank,” whose leads face the brutal antagonism of members of an Evangelical church.

Latin America’s – and Huelva’s – Issue Cinema

“We attempt to program films which have a variety of themes, such as might be LGBTQ, mourning or mental health, subjects which can engage and concern society,” says Martín. Huelva’s lineup, as so much of Latin American cinema, deals with issues. In Latin America, the issues are impossible to ignore, Nobel Prize Winning novelist Mario Vargas Llosa said decades ago. That remains true, however, down to this day.

‘Dept. Q’ Crew Member Dies on Set in Edinburgh After Season 2 Wrapped Filming

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

A crew member working on the Netflix series “Dept. Q” has died on set in Edinburgh after filming wrapped for Season 2. The production company behind the series, Left Bank Pictures, said it is “deeply shocked and saddened by the tragic death.”

The man has not been named, and specific details about the incident have yet to be reported. Police were called to FirstStage Studios in Scotland on Thursday afternoon after the crew member was seriously injured. Emergency services responded to the incident, but the man ultimately died. Related Stories Selena Gomez and Demi Lovato Recreate ‘Princess Protection Program’ Poster in New Rare Beauty Campaign ‘Lords of War’ Trailer: Nicolas Cage and Bill Skarsgård Star as Father and Son in ‘Lord of War’ Sequel

The death took place after cameras stopped rolling, meaning the incident could have taken place during the dismantling of sets. Scottish police confirmed that the death is not being treated as suspicious, “however, inquiries are ongoing to establish the full circumstances.”

“We are deeply shocked and saddened by the tragic death of a member of our ‘Dept. Q’ crew,” a spokesperson for Left Bank Pictures said in a statement. “Our thoughts are with their family, friends and colleagues at this incredibly difficult time, and we are offering support for those affected.”

Representatives for Netflix did not immediately respond to Variety‘s request for comment.

A popular and award-nominated crime series, “Dept. Q” is one of Netflix’s biggest international hits in the past few years. The show stars Matthew Goode as emotionally scarred detective Carl Morck, who returns to police work months after a traumatizing injury. The first season aired on Netflix in May 2025 and was renewed for a second season a few months later.

The Moment I Saw This Gorgeous Lake Como Airbnb, I Knew I Had to Plan a Trip to Perledo

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Maybe it’s because I’m a dreamer, or maybe it's because I’m slightly impulsive, but whenever I'm planning a trip, I start by browsing Airbnb. I find it the easiest way to envision my time away, and I dropped the notion a long time ago that you don’t actually spend time in your accommodations. That's how I ended up finding myself in a teeny town called Perledo this past summer—my mom had always dreamed of visiting Lake Como for her birthday, so I decided to see what great accommodations I could find in the area for a visit in August, her birth month.

For a stay in Italy, if it’s sleek, minimalist, or modern, keep it away from me. I’m drawn to homes that feel like a reflection of the country I’m in—something uncommon with character and a story. So when I found this unbelievably charming rental in Perledo—a quiet village in the hills above Varenna, overlooking Lake Como—on the Airbnb platform, I booked it for my mom, sister, and I before we even had flights. I mean, look at it!

My family and I arrived in Lake Como on a scorching August day after spending seven days in Sardinia. After a flight, bus, train, and short taxi ride up and around the mountain, we were thrilled to land in our new home. A few days before our arrival, our host, Giulia (having the same name as her felt like a good omen to me!), helped us navigate getting to the apartment from the train, offering us multiple numbers for local taxis, as we hadn't wanted to rent a car. (I'm glad we went that route, because I wouldn't have wanted to deal with parking.)

Upon entering the home, after several trips up and down the stairs, we threw down our bags and immediately made our way onto the balcony. The sunlight beamed through the French doors with the mountains in the background, and the smell of crisp Alpine air made the three of us pause. Staring at the boats as they glided through the water, we all immediately forgot about the long journey it took to get there.

We discovered a bottle of red wine, taralli, and a bunch of Nespresso pods waiting for us on the kitchen table, and I was delighted to find the pantry filled with essentials—salt, pepper, olive oil, and vinegar. You know, those items you might need to complete a meal or a snack, but are annoying to buy on vacation.

The apartment felt warm from the sunlight, but also by design. The pearly walls, with green cabinets and warm wood accents, felt very grounding, like an extension of the atmosphere outside. The tile flooring in the living area reminded me of my cousin's house in Rome, and the pistachio-green-framed window in the bathroom opened wide for a clear picture of the mountains and lake.

After already spending a week with my mom and sister, I was ready for some space, so I opted for the sofa bed with a memory foam topper in the living room. The bed was already made, so it was easy to slip into the soft sheets for a nap after my shower. Throughout the stay, we were mindful not to let the hot water meter fall below a certain level, per Giulia's advice, but between the three of us, we rarely came close.

Giulia also gave us tons of information about the area, including ferry schedules, walking paths, and numbers for hairstylists (I'm always looking for a good blowout). Being able to communicate with her as a local resource felt like we had a personal tour guide with us at all times.

When we left the apartment on our first evening, we followed Giulia’s instructions to catch the complimentary Perledo Night Bus, a service provided by the town from 7 p.m. till midnight, to get to nearby Varenna, where we could grab the ferry to take us to other towns. Walking up and down the mountain, especially at night, would have been challenging and slightly dangerous, so we were happy to grab the shuttle, which was a 10-minute walk from our place.

Unlike Como town or Bellagio, Varenna is quieter, with restaurants that shut down around 9 p.m. Luckily, there are some places to eat and drink in Perledo too. For example, Bar Milano is where locals congregate for their morning espressos or evening nightcaps. For us, it was where we would grab our bus rides up and down the mountain, as well as breakfast (the apricot croissants are incredible).

The walk to Bar Milano was a deeply scenic experience for us—the sunlight glistening on the lake to our right; the lush green mountains dotted with homes to our left. We often noticed fountains and shrines to pray at on our journeys up and down, something consistent on most streets in the village. After arriving home with our breakfast every day, we would start our mornings slow, sitting on the balcony with our espressos and cornetti, taking in the postcard-perfect views of the lake. We often said hi to our neighbors just below us, families who were keen on enjoying the morning air too.

We made few plans for our days, except a reservation for my mom’s birthday lunch. We spent four hours indulging at Passalacqua, the boutique hotel in Moltrasio, and between the beauty of the space, the unhurried pace of the meal, and the kindness of the staff, it’s a memory we will all keep forever. Other afternoons were spent ferry hopping, exploring different towns. In Bellagio, it was imperative we walked down the most famous corridor—Salita Serbelloni. Filled with small artisanal shops and restaurants, it was here I bought a rosary necklace from a man named Filippo, the owner of Bellagio New Wave, a family business that has been in operation for over 100 years. He told me that he grew up above the building.

After days spent on our feet, ears buzzing from the large crowds, nothing was more serene than returning to the quiet of our apartment. Stepping off the shuttle and onto the street for our walk back to the Airbnb brought a sense of tranquility over our three days there, like we were truly going home. Being able to stare into the night sky, overlooking the stillness of the water while being hit with a slight breeze, offered a respite from the adventures of the day.

When I think of Lake Como, I think of two things: romance and luxury. Away from the noise but still close enough to the action, this Airbnb gave us a slice of that fantasy. I now recommend Perdolo to everyone. Safe to say, I’ll continue building my trips around great Airbnbs. The rest always figures itself out later.

As the most discerning, up-to-the-minute voice in all things travel, Condé Nast Traveler is the global citizen’s bible and muse, offering both inspiration and vital intel. We understand that time is the greatest luxury, which is why Condé Nast Traveler mines its network of experts and influencers so that you never waste a meal, a drink, or a hotel stay wherever you are in the world.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/providers/ardent-health-acquiring-missouri-nonprofit-boone-health-47m" hreflang="en">Ardent Health acquiring Missouri nonprofit Boone Health for $47M</a>

📅 2026-11-13  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →
Ardent Health acquiring Missouri nonprofit Boone Health for $47M" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Ardent Health is acquiring Boone Health, a mid-sized nonprofit system based in Columbia, Missouri, for $47 million in cash, the organizations announced Thursday. It marks Ardent's first entry into the Missouri healthcare market.

The deal, expected to close in the first quarter of 2027, includes a 10-year, $200 million capital commitment on nonprofit Ardent’s part. That money will be invested in Boone’s facilities, workforce and, in the near term, an expansion of its emergency department, according to the announcement.

Ardent has agreed to maintain Boone’s essential hospital services while developing its 392-bed regional referral center, Boone Hospital Center. It also plans to maintain employment for existing Boone employees and providers, continue its charity care policy and establish a local advisory board.

“We are acquiring a strong asset in an attractive and growing midsize market with significant opportunity to drive operating and financial improvement, all while delivering high patient quality care and safety,” Ardent Health President and CEO Dave Caspers said during a Thursday afternoon investor call discussing the deal. “It also represents an effective use of capital at a compelling valuation that we expect will create long-term shareholder value.”

Alongside its main hospital, Ardent would acquire Boone’s medical group, 19 care sites including an outpatient campus and five medical office buildings, related ancillary services and real estate. The system is the second-largest provider in its region with about 30% market share and half a billion dollars of annual revenue, Caspers said, with a local population that is well insured (93% coverage) and growing well ahead of the national average (3.4% in the next five years).

“Boone expands our footprint into a new attractive market,” Caspers explained. “It diversifies our business and enhances scale. Further, adding Boone's regional system to our portfolio is consistent with our strategy of building leading healthcare platforms in growing mid-sized communities. We're highly aligned with Boone Health on culture and mission, including putting the patient at the center of everything we do, with an aim toward best-in-class clinical care, patient quality, safety and access to services.”

The executive noted to investors that Boone’s facilities are relatively modern and well maintained, allowing Ardent to direct more capital toward its growth. The company has also identified “meaningful opportunities to optimize financial performance.” By leveraging its larger scale and expertise to tackle costs (supply chain procurement, vendor consolidation) and revenue cycle processes, Ardent executives said they expect to generate about $5 million to $10 million additional EBITDA within the deal’s first year, and at least $30 million of annual synergies three years after the deal’s close.

Boone Health has been independent since 2021, following over three decades during which it operated as part of the BJC HealthCare system. In 2025, its leadership kicked off a search for potential strategic partners “that could provide additional capital and resources to support the system's continued growth. Ardent was selected after a comprehensive evaluation process led by an independent advisory firm,” according to Thursday’s announcement.

“In Ardent, we identified a partner that supports our mission, respects our culture and can accelerate our vision without compromising what makes Boone Health special,” Jerry Kennett, M.D., chair of Boone’s Board of Trustees, said in the announcement. "Following a thoughtful review, the Board is confident Ardent is the right long-term partner for Boone Health. This agreement protects what our community has built while giving Boone Health the resources it needs to grow."

Ardent Health’s current operations include 30 acute care hospitals and over 280 care sites, several of which are run as part of a joint venture structure with other health system partners. In 2025, the company increased its revenues by 6% to $6.3 billion and grew its adjusted EBITDA to $545 million.

The company recently logged a strong third quarter off the back of solid demand for emergent care and progress on its ongoing performance improvement initiative. In September, it also officially divested from a joint venture with the University of Kansas Health System, a decision that leadership said is part of an ongoing review of its portfolio to ensure long-term strategic fit among its hospitals and partnerships.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/regulatory/policy-brief-trump-admin-awards-10b-rhtp-funds-year-two-hhs-invests-97m-preventing-elder" hreflang="en">Policy Brief: Trump admin unveils $10B in second-year rural health awards; HHS invests $9.7M to combat elder abuse</a>

📅 2026-11-13  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →
Policy Brief: Trump admin unveils $10B in second-year rural health awards; HHS invests $9.7M to combat elder abuse" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Stay up to date on the latest across Washington, D.C. with this brief. Read below for news from Nov. 2 to 13.

HHS announced a $9.7 million investment Monday through the Administration for Community Living to help prevent elder abuse, neglect and exploitation.

Financial exploitation alone is estimated to cost older Americans $28.3 billion annually, according to the department.

The funding will, among other things, support expanding legal help and strengthening advocacy for those in nursing homes and other care facilities. The ACL will award six new Elder Justice Innovation grants over the next three years totaling $5.6 million and four new Legal Assistance Enhancement Program grants totaling $1.9 million over two years.

“Older Americans have earned the right to live with dignity, control their own lives, and protect what they worked hard to build,” said Robert F. Kennedy, Jr., HHS secretary, in a statement. “The Trump Administration is investing more than $9.7 million to prevent abuse and financial exploitation, expand access to legal help, and defend the rights of nursing home residents. We are putting practical tools and trusted support into communities to help older Americans safeguard their independence and financial security.”

The Trump administration announced Monday funding for the second year of the Rural Health Transformation Program (RHTP), with $10 billion in awards slated for all 50 states.

Similar to fiscal year 2026, Texas and Alaska will receive the largest amount of funding as part of the program. Texas is set to receive $281,976,026, while Alaska will receive $272,653,149.

New Jersey will receive the lowest amount of funding at $142,338,224. Full story.

HHS announced Friday a notice of funding opportunity (NOFO) for its Women’s Initiative for Screening and Health (WISH) program.

The program will award up to $1 million in fiscal year 2027 to establish a multidisciplinary coalition to update HHS’ Health Resources and Services Administration (HRSA)-supported Women’s Preventive Services Guidelines.

The funding will fund one organization to create a multidisciplinary panel of at least 10 experts from women’s health, primary care, behavioral health and more. It will also allow the organization to conduct evidence reviews and develop and disseminate information on HRSA guidelines.

“The latest research only makes a difference when it reaches women and the clinicians caring for them,” said Tom Engels, HRSA Administrator, in a statement. “By bringing together clinical expertise and trusted community partners, we can help more women access recommended preventive care nationwide.”

The department says the opportunity advances priorities outlined in an October executive order from President Donald Trump.

CMS plans to trim several “unnecessary” requirements for hospitals and other providers participating in Medicare, including emergency services readiness requirements and staff training for obstetrical services, pushed through during the tail end of the Biden administration.

The proposed rule’s Conditions of Participation (CoP) rollbacks, shared Nov. 5, followed a “comprehensive review” CMS conducted to identify and eliminate “obsolete, outdated and excessively burdensome regulations.” Full story.

CMS finalized its annual outpatient hospital payments rule with a lower-than-proposed rate bump and its major, industry-opposed policy shifts moderated, but largely intact.

The 2027 Hospital Outpatient Prospective Payment (OPPS) and Ambulatory Surgical Center (ASC) Payment Systems rule outlines a 2.3% reimbursement increase for those meeting applicable quality reporting requirements. That’s below the proposed 2.4% increase and reflects a 3.2% projected hospital market basket increase and a 0.9 percentage point productivity adjustment. Full story.

CMS finalized sweeping Medicare payment reforms it says will shift the program from volume-based "sick care" toward prevention, primary care and accountable care.

The 2027 Medicare Physician Fee Schedule rule includes a roughly 2% reduction in baseline physician payment rates with targeted increases and new payment opportunities for accountable care, primary and chronic care and behavioral health. Full story.

CMS and HHS announced the finalization of a rule that strengthens oversight of Organ Procurement Organizations (OPO), building on a 2019 Trump executive order.

Key provisions of the final rule include:

“Americans waiting for lifesaving transplants cannot afford poor performance by OPOs, and CMS is committed to holding these organizations accountable,” said Mehmet Oz, M.D., CMS administrator, in a statement. “With today’s new rule, CMS is building on the success of organ procurement reforms from the first Trump Administration to promote clearer documentation, higher donation rates, and swifter consequences for bad actors.”

The Federal Trade Commission sent warning letters to 25 companies that conduct marketing or generate sales leads for insurers as ACA enrollment season gets underway.

The commission said in an announcement that the letters aim to put these companies on notice about deceptive or unfair marketing tactics that are likely unlawful. Full story.

Could Firenze Lai's first UK solo show signal a shift in global market recognition for Hong Kong artists?

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Firenze Lai’s A Second Storm (2025) from her White Cube show Photo: © White Cube/Theo Christelis; © the artist

In mid-October, coinciding with Frieze London and nearly four years after she relocated from her native Hong Kong to London, the artist Firenze Lai opened her first UK solo exhibition at White Cube Mason’s Yard. Featuring new works created in her adopted home, North and True North (until 14 November) is not just a milestone for her career, it also signals a potential shift in major galleries’ approach to Asia.

Lai is among a handful of Hong Kong artists represented by major Western blue-chip galleries today. Born in 1984 and educated in Hong Kong, the artist has exhibited internationally, including in the main exhibition of the 57th Venice Biennale in 2017. Her current show marks the first solo exhibition White Cube has staged by an artist originating from Hong Kong since it opened its outpost in the city in 2012.

The shift suggests the maturity of this generation of mid-career artists from Hong Kong, several of whom have relocated abroad fairly recently. It also raises hopes that Western blue-chip galleries are finally adopting a less siloed approach to Asia, in which global art markets were approached from a simplistic geographical perspective. This mindset of “keeping Asians in Asia” kept transnational relationships rare, the cultural strategist Adeline Ooi notes, but this is starting to change.

“People are much more cosmopolitan and international today. They are not just rooted within their region and bounded by their geographies,” says Ooi, the former director for Asia at Art Basel.

Major international galleries are taking a more sophisticated approach, rather than simply placing Asian artists in front of Asian collectors, says the Taipei- and New York-based art adviser Gladys Lin. “It’s about integrating that artist into the wider language of the gallery programme, so that collectors from different backgrounds can understand the work in relation to other artists, histories and conversations.”

Hong Kong has long been Asia’s biggest art market hub, with headline-grabbing auction sales for over half a century joined, since the 2000s, by a lively landscape of galleries and fairs catering to local collectors, and those from mainland China and the wider region. The city has hosted Art Basel Hong Kong since 2013, and Asian outposts of blue-chip galleries proliferated after the launch of the ArtHK fair in 2008. Artists from the city, however, have struggled to receive much attention in the global market, or even at home.

Leading multinational Western blue-chip galleries such as White Cube, Hauser & Wirth, David Zwirner, Gagosian and Pace have shown Asian artists internationally, but they tend to concentrate on bolstering their rosters with established names from Asian cultural powerhouses like mainland China, South Korea and Japan.

Firenze Lai, who has lived in London since 2022 © White Cube/Ollie Hammick

Hongkonger artists are increasingly exhibiting abroad, but primarily with smaller galleries. The Taipei-based artist Lee Kit works with galleries including Massimo de Carlo in London and ShugoArts in Tokyo. The UK-based Chris Huen Sin-kan showed with the now-defunct Simon Lee in New York and London, and more recently with Matt Carey-Williams.

While diaspora and émigré Hongkongers have more opportunities to cultivate overseas networks, artists based in the city are also widening their reach. Chang Ya Chin held her first solo show with Perrotin in New York in 2025, while Stephen Wong Chun-hei exhibited with Unit London in 2022 and Mother’s Tankstation in 2025, which also represents Lee Kit. The sound artist Samson Young performed his work Canon at Art Basel Unlimited in 2016, presented by Cologne’s Galerie Gisela Capitain and the now-closed Team Gallery. Wong Ping held a solo show at Tanya Bonakdar Gallery in New York in 2021, while Mak2 had her European gallery solo debut in 2023 with the now-defunct Peres Projects in Berlin.

However, representation by one of the global “big five” galleries remains rare for Hong Kong artists. White Cube began working with Lai in July 2025. “Her paintings have a singularity that speaks to the human condition, a universal theme that audiences everywhere can appreciate,” a spokesperson for the gallery says.

Lai was born and raised in Hong Kong, and has lived in London since 2022. Her visual language nonetheless remains rooted in her lived experience of her densely populated hometown. “We are not 100% Chinese, nor are we 100% British,” Lai says.

Moving to London changed how she approaches the canvas. The painter recalls that during her time in Hong Kong—where she built a solid career and a following for her dreamlike figurative paintings through a long-term and continuing collaboration with Guangzhou gallery Vitamin Creative Space—she tended to work on smaller canvases.

“After moving to London, I felt that I needed a bigger canvas. I have a lot more to say,” Lai says. Much of her poignant new work draws from deep emotional responses to her changing environment before and after relocating.

Lai says the current timing for global market recognition for Hong Kong artists makes sense. Those of her generation, born between the late 1970s and 90s, needed time to establish themselves and explore which galleries would be a good fit for their career. “I am proud of the fact that Hong Kong has produced a generation of artists who can have solo shows in London,” she says. “I’ve been told to give up many times. But I can now tell a 15-year-old [aspiring artist] that this is possible.”

It remains unclear how much long-term benefit a solo exhibition at a global gallery can bring to an artist’s career. “Who are the people paying attention to Firenze right now? If they are private collectors, who are they and what are their demographics? If it’s an institution, who are they?” Ooi asks. Expanding the following for her work will take time as much as exposure.

Lin says Lai’s move to London put her into a different art world, where she offers galleries a “compelling combination”. “She already comes with a collector base in Asia, an institutional history and more than a decade of critical exposure,” Lin says. “It’s not simply about bringing in Asian collectors, but about adding an artist who connects different collectors, institutional and geographical systems. For a global gallery today, geography can still be a useful entry point, but it cannot be the final framework.”

The new event is supported by the public Mega Ace Fund, which subsidises large cultural projects in the Special Administrative Region

Travel restrictions continue to prevent the physical presence of many international exhibitors at the fair, with 75 galleries opting for satellite booths

Opportunity knocks, but there is a risk that it will deliver a blow to local artists and gallerists

Sotheby’s tests the waters with first oil painting at auction in Hong Kong

Investing - Theory, News &amp; General • Re: Article: Do Bond Investors Get Rewarded for Taking Dur. Risk? (P. 1) Edward F. McQuarrie, William J. Bernstein, 11/6

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

A dissent, based on a different objective. As a retiree, I hold Muni bond funds for INCOME, with no plans to ever sell. As one might expect, longer-duration funds like VWLUX produce more income over 1, 5, and 10 years than intermediate-duration funds such as VWIUX. Rising interest rates drive down the market value of VWLUX but have little effect on the income produced in the short term - each bond is a contract to yield a certain regular interest rate and pay back principal at maturity, and this is not influenced by fluctuations in market value. Moreover, as bonds mature and are replaced, the new bonds have higher yields if interest rates have been rising.

I suspect that quite a few older investors are in a similar situation, concerned only with the income produced. Comments and corrections welcome.
Your premise isn't necessarily true. As interest rates rise, the yield of shorter-term bond funds rises much faster than the yield of long-term bond funds. How can that be? Because the shorter-term bond fund replaces its maturing, or shortest term, bonds much faster, which raises its yield at a faster pace. Take a look at the yields of bond funds in the 1970s as an example. Short-term bond fund yields rose to nearly 17% and long-term bond fund yields were only in the low teens. Intermediate-term bond funds were a better overall compromise.

New York Giants call for 'White Out' at MetLife Stadium vs. Commanders

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Thursday night's game against the Washington Commanders should be like a homecoming game for the NFC East-leading New York Giants.

The team will wear its throwback 1980s all-white uniforms and has encouraged fans to do the same. They are calling it the Giants' "White Out."

The game will stream on Amazon Prime for the world to see as John Harbaugh's 5-2-1 Giants head into the second half of the 2026 season, focused on winning their first division title in 15 years.

This is an important divisional game, and the Giants aren't looking past the 2-5-1 Commanders, who are calling this a "must-win."

"We want to go 1-0, but this game is more than 1-0. It's 2-0, just because the last game ended out in a tie that we should have had," Giants wide receiver Malik Nabers said on Tuesday. "So, both of us are fighting to get this game. We're trying to go 2-0 since we lost the last one, well, since we finished off in a tie last time, which we should have won. But this game is the same as importance as every game, we're going to try to go out there and give our best shot to get this dub."

"We definitely don't want to let 'em down, man," running back Devin Singletary told the New York Post. "Want to go out there and handle business."

"It's gonna be a lot of fun," Singletary added. "I know it's gonna be rocking. I know they're gonna come out and show out for us. Hopefully they make a lot of noise and make it hard on the other team."

"It'd be awesome if the whole stadium was white, and it was a nice dark day with lights on, it'd be pretty cool," running back Cam Skattebo told The Post. "I've never been a part of a whiteout myself, nor have I played against a whiteout. I can't wait to see what the Giants fans bring." It will also be quarterback J.J. McCarthy's first home start. Although he's used to big crowds, having played his college ball at Michigan, he is in for a treat as the Giants fan base is stoked.

"He'll be ready," Harbaugh said of McCarthy. "He's played in a lot of big stadiums before, and he'll love our fans. I can tell him that. I can tell him that. He's going to love our fans."

McCarthy was asked what it's going to be like to run onto that stadium for the first time as the starting quarterback for the New York Football Giants.

"I haven't thought about it until now, but I'm just so blessed to be a part of this team," he told reporters Tuesday. "I say it over and over again, and I just can't thank the Giants organization and the fanbase for welcoming me. At the end of the day, it's running out there to do my job at a high level and go get a win on Thursday night. That's the only thing I care about, so just being able to embrace that, soak it up but at the end of the day, it's about that first play."

The Giants haven't played a meaningful game in many years, especially at home. Time to change that.

"This New York fanbase, especially with the Knicks winning this last year," Skattebo said, "so being able to see the Giants continue to win and hopefully come out with a championship at the end of it would be pretty special."

Mike Tomlin highlights 12 top coaching candidates for 2027 NFL offseason

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The 2026 NFL season has reached its midpoint, and all 32 coaches who began the season in charge of their respective franchises remain in place.

As every NFL fan knows, that will not last long. Last season, 10 of the league's 32 teams made coaching changes. NFL owners are growing ever-impatient as they look to quickly build Super Bowl contenders, so leashes are shorter than ever for coaches.

That's why many will be on scorching hot seats during the second half of the season. And while none have been dismissed yet, a couple may fail to finish out the season, while numerous others will see their tenures end on "Black Monday," the day after the final game of the regular season.

But who will NFL owners and decision-makers be eyeing among the league's next crop of top coaching candidates? A few prominent retreads are on the market, along with the usual plethora of young, offensive-minded coaches.

Here's a look at the top coaching candidates to watch as the 2027 NFL coach hiring cycle draws ever nearer.

Week 3: Denver Broncos cheerleaders perform during the "Sunday Night Football" game against the Los Angeles Rams at Empower Field at Mile High.

Week 3: The Maracanã Stadium gets set to host the International Series game between the Baltimore Ravens and Dallas Cowboys in Rio de Janeiro, Brazil.

Week 3: Corgi dog competitions take place during halftime of the game between the Detroit Lions and New York Jets at Ford Field.

Week 3: A fan looks on during warmups prior to the game between the Cleveland Browns and Carolina Panthers at Huntington Bank Field in Cleveland, Ohio.

Week 2: Singer Taylor Swift watches the game between the Indianapolis Colts and Kansas City Chiefs at Arrowhead Stadium.

Week 2: The Canadian and U.S. anthems are sung during a pregame ceremony before the "Thursday Night Football" game between Detroit Lions and Buffalo Bills at new Highmark Stadium.

Week 1: Fans arrive ahead of the NFL game between the Los Angeles Rams and San Francisco 49ers at the Melbourne Cricket Ground (in Melbourne, Australia. The 49ers won the first NFL game played in Australia, 27-7.

Week 1: Kim Thayil of Soundgarden and Mike McCready of Pearl Jam perform during halftime of the 2026 NFL Kickoff Game between the Seattle Seahawks and New England Patriots at Lumen Field.

Tomlin is easily the biggest name on the NFL's coaching carousel for 2027. The 54-year-old led the Steelers to a 193-114-2 record across 19 seasons in Pittsburgh and won a Super Bowl while in charge of the franchise. He famously never had a losing season, and his steady performance will certainly endear him to NFL organizations looking for a proven culture-builder to improve their operations.

The lone question is whether Tomlin will want to return to the sideline after stepping down from the Steelers following the 2025 NFL season. He is currently working as a studio analyst for NBC and could be happy to remain in that role moving forward.

Unlike Tomlin, McDermott's intentions are known. The 52-year-old wants another crack at being an NFL head coach after leading the Bills to a 98-50 regular-season record across nine seasons in Buffalo.

McDermott is a strong, defensive-minded coach and has shown an ability to bring in quality offensive coordinators, with Joe Brady being among the best hires of his tenure. His track record leading the Bills to consistent success – eight playoff appearances in nine seasons – should appeal to NFL owners, even though McDermott was never quite able to guide any of his Bills teams to the Super Bowl.

McDermott will also be helped in the interview process by the fact that he is not currently employed by an NFL team. That will allow any team that fires their coach in-season to immediately check in with McDermott and gauge where he stands among the top candidates on the market.

The Vikings started the 2026 NFL season with a 6-2 record despite sporting an offense that ranks 30th in EPA per play, per the NFL's Next Gen Stats. Their success has largely been thanks to Flores' defense, which has established itself as the league's best.

Flores' aggressive, blitz-heavy attack has helped Minnesota post the NFL's best pressure rate while ranking second league-wide in defensive EPA per play behind only the Tampa Bay Buccaneers. The Vikings are surrendering a league-low 14.5 points per game and proved, by containing Josh Allen on "Monday Night Football" in Week 9, that they can be a championship-caliber defense.

With that in mind, any NFL organization looking to improve its defense should be interested in bringing Flores aboard. His system has proven transferable across all of his NFL stops, so he is practically guaranteed to turn whatever team he coaches into a top-10 defense.

That said, Flores is still embroiled in a discrimination lawsuit against the NFL, and it isn't clear whether that legal process will make any teams apprehensive about hiring him.

Udinski is just 30 years old, but he's a rising star in the coaching ranks. He has helped the Jaguars become one of the NFL's best and most efficient offenses during the 2026 season, and his ability to scheme players open should make him a hot commodity on the coaching market.

Udinski would be one of the NFL's youngest-ever coaches, but NFL owners have been more willing than ever to follow the Sean McVay blueprint and take chances on young coaches. That could give Udinski a path toward earning a coaching job even with just seven years of NFL coaching experience.

LERNER: He drew up plays at 5. At 30, Jaguars OC Grant Udinski is one of the NFL's hottest coaches

Doyle is in a similar boat to Udinski. He's just 30 years old but has enjoyed a rapid rise over the last two seasons, finding success working as Ben Johnson's offensive coordinator in 2025 before getting a chance to call his own plays with the Ravens this season.

Doyle's ties to Johnson, who has rapidly turned the Bears into a Super Bowl contender, should make him a popular interview during the 2027 coach hiring cycle. He could also improve his candidacy if he's able to effectively lead the Ravens through injuries to quarterbacks Lamar Jackson and Tyler Huntley and give Baltimore a chance to make a playoff push with Jacoby Brissett at quarterback.

Brothers Matt LaFleur and Mike LaFleur are both NFL coaches. Could Klay Kubiak and Klint Kubiak be next? The latter Kubiak brother has performed well in his first season with the Las Vegas Raiders while the former has been a part of Kyle Shanahan's staff for six seasons.

Kubiak has performed well since being elevated to offensive coordinator in 2025. He led the 49ers to a top-10 offense in both scoring and yardage in his first season in the role and is on pace to do the same in 2026. He has helped Shanahan guide San Francisco to success amid a plethora of injuries at receiver while Brock Purdy has thrived under his tutelage, completing 69.2% of his passes for 4,147 yards, 37 touchdowns and 15 interceptions in 17 starts.

Purdy's success should be enough to get Kubiak interviews; and if NFL teams get the sense he can develop into a quarterback whisperer, they may pull the trigger on hiring him.

Many believed Scheelhaase might earn a coaching job during last year's hiring cycle. At the time, he was coming off a successful season as the Rams' pass game coordinator and had helped lead Matthew Stafford to an MVP season.

Scheelhaase wasn't ultimately hired, but he earned a promotion to the Rams' offensive coordinator role. The Rams have gotten off to a disappointing 5-4 start, but their offense still ranks fourth in yards per game and ninth in EPA per play, per the NFL's Next Gen Stats. That, plus his ties to Sean McVay, will be enough to get Scheelhaase more looks during the 2027 cycle.

It's also worth noting Scheelhaase was a college quarterback at Illinois, so he has experience playing the position. That could prove valuable for teams looking for a coach who can develop a young quarterback into a star-caliber player.

Scheelhaase isn't the only Rams assistant who should be a hot commodity. Shula got plenty of interviews last season after leading the Rams to a top-10 mark in defensive EPA per play and has only continued to raise his stock in 2026.

The Rams presently rank fourth in defensive EPA per play and have allowed the sixth-fewest yards and eighth-fewest points this season. Shula has achieved those results despite missing star edge rusher Myles Garrett for most of the season, and he has helped develop players like Nate Landman into key pieces of the team's defense.

Also, for those wondering, yes: Chris Shula is related to Don Shula. He is the grandson of the legendary Miami Dolphins coach.

Durde has spent the last three seasons as Seattle's defensive coordinator under Mike Macdonald. He has helped Macdonald turn the Seahawks into one of the league's best defenses amid their run to a Super Bowl 60 win over the New England Patriots.

The Seahawks had the NFL's top-scoring defense last season and have allowed the fewest yards thus far in 2026. That will help Durde get looks during this coaching cycle, and so will his and Macdonald's abilities to coach up their young players – like former undrafted free agents Ty Okada and Drake Thomas – and turn them into key role players.

Campanile has spent the last two seasons as the Jaguars' defensive coordinator. He has created a defensive identity in Jacksonville, one focused on attacking downhill and stopping the run. That has paid dividends with the Jaguars allowing just 89.3 rushing yards per game in 2026, fifth-fewest in the league.

NFL owners looking for a hard-nosed, defensive-minded leader could find themselves gravitating toward Campanile. His aggressive defensive style figures to transfer well to his next landing spot, provided he has the personnel needed to stop the run.

The Falcons rank sixth in pressure rate during the 2026 NFL season. This is despite their top pressure-generators being the little-known duo of Brandon Dorlus (29) and Cameron Thomas (26).

Those pressure numbers are a feather-in-the-cap of Ulbrich, who has done well to help an undermanned unit perform at a higher-than-expected level. That's not surprising, given the 49-year-old frequently got the most out of the defenses he coached with the Jets as part of Robert Saleh's staff from 2021-24.

All told, there's a reason the Falcons were adamant about keeping Ulbrich as their defensive coordinator even after switching coaches following the 2026 season. His motivational skills are strong, and his ability to develop defensive talent should earn him interviews this offseason.

Graham has spent eight seasons as an NFL defensive coordinator for four different teams. He is finally enjoying high-end success with the Steelers, who have helped lead to a top-seven mark in yards allowed, points allowed and defensive EPA per play.

Graham, 47, has earned several head coaching interviews during his career and figures to once again draw interest this offseason. His experience across six different NFL organizations and a Super Bowl ring with the Patriots as a defensive assistant could eventually be enough to get him his first chance to be an NFL head coach.

Ex-Serie A flop Jardel opens up on drug abuse: ‘I nearly died’

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Former Ancona striker Mario Jardel opens up on his alcohol and drug abuse: ‘I nearly died; a friend of mine saved my life.’

The former Brazilian striker played for Ancona in Serie A in the second half of the 2003-04 campaign. He joined the Italian club from Bolton in the January transfer window.

“I only have bad memories. In six months, I didn’t get paid, and I played just three matches,” the ex-striker told La Gazzetta dello Sport about his brief spell in Ancona.

“The squad was packed full of players, poorly run, and went bankrupt at the end of the year. I bonded with Sonetti and Galeone, much less so with the fans.

“I was 31, I was overweight, and someone called me ‘Lardel’. Probably because when they unveiled me against Perugia I saluted the wrong end: we had the same colours. Some fans took offence after that gesture,” the former striker added.

“Jorge Mendes. He wasn’t my agent, but a close friend,” Jardel replied.

“He had contacts in Italy, mentioned my name to some agents and somehow they brought me to Ancona. I was looking for a club where I could play regularly and recover after the nightmare of missing out on the 2002 World Cup, but mentally I never recovered.”

Between 2001 and 2003, Jardel had scored 67 goals in 62 appearances with Sporting CP, still not enough to earn a call-up to the Brazil national team for the 2002 World Cup, which the South Americans won.

“I fell into depression and started drinking excessively,” Jardel admitted.

“Playing in a World Cup was the dream of my life. My first wife left me, and there were issues regarding the custody of our children. I felt that everything was slipping away from me, but the worst was yet to come.”

But the ex-Serie A flop hadn’t hit rock bottom yet.

“In the summer of 2003, I tried cocaine. I was on holiday, and I knew I could take it during that period because there was no doping control,” he said.

“I liked it; it made me feel alive and not depressed. Once, I stayed awake for an entire week. Then I had an overdose; I nearly died, and a friend who was with me saved my life. That was where I hit rock bottom.

“I spent months speaking with psychiatrists,” he continued.

“I came out of it for good when I met my second wife, Sandra: she made me understand the meaning of life, and today I’m happy.”

The former has returned to Brazil and is an investor.

“In Fortaleza, the property market is very popular, and it’s a good way to earn money. In my spare time, I enjoy the sun on the beach, although I miss Italy,” he concluded.

Lorenzo Bettoni is the Editor of Football Italia.

Investing $196 Per Week in This ETF Could Build a $1 Million Portfolio. Here's How.

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Time can do a lot of the heavy lifting for you.

For investors saving up for retirement, hitting the million-dollar mark is a huge financial milestone to be proud of. It takes a lot of work. Surprisingly, it doesn't actually require saving a million bucks, which would be very difficult for many investors.

Thanks to the power of compounding interest, an investor can potentially hit the million-dollar-saved mark by investing just $196 weekly in an S&P 500 (^GSPC +1.31%) exchange-traded fund (ETF) like the Vanguard S&P 500 ETF (VOO +1.28%).

Since it began trading in September 2010, the VOO ETF has averaged 12.5% annual returns (14.6% when dividends are included). That's above-average growth, so it might be better to calculate returns using the S&P 500's long-term return average of 10%. Assuming it averages a more modest 10% annually, $196 invested weekly ($10,192 annually) could easily grow to $1 million in 25 years.

That's the power of compounding interest.

If VOO maintains its 12.5% average (which is unlikely but not impossible), an investor could hit the $1 million mark in a little under 22 years. If dividends are reinvested and the 14.6% total return is used, the $1 million mark is reached in just over 20 years.

Past results don't guarantee future performance, so you never want to assume anything when it comes to potential returns. Historically, the S&P 500 has always grown over the long term, and that's likely to continue. However, only time will tell what that growth looks like (if at all).

Either way, the most important thing you can do as an investor is to stay consistent and let time do much of the heavy lifting. There will be inevitable rough patches along the way, but VOO is made up of roughly 500 of the world's most successful and promising companies. It doesn't make it foolproof, but it does put the odds in your favor.

Scientists Ranked 20 Protein-Rich Foods on Health, Affordability, and Sustainability. One Surprising Food Came Out on Top

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Tofu took the crown in a showdown of 20 protein-rich foods judged on health, affordability, availability, and their toll on the planet.

Protein-rich foods can look very different when cost, access, health, and environmental impact all enter the picture. In a study published in Frontiers in Sustainable Food Systems, Dr. Chris Macdonald developed a framework that combines these four considerations to compare commonly consumed protein sources.

Among the 20 foods assessed, tofu took the top spot, while cheddar cheese finished last. The results followed a clear pattern: plant-based foods occupied the entire highest-scoring fifth, and animal-based products made up the lowest-scoring fifth.

Tofu’s lead came from strong results across both parts of the assessment. It earned a perfect 16 points for environmental performance, placing in the best-performing fifth for greenhouse gas emissions, water pollution, land use, and freshwater use. It also scored 20 out of 24 points for health and accessibility, including full marks for providing all nine essential amino acids.

Together, these measures explain why the rankings go beyond protein content alone. They weigh the environmental costs of producing protein alongside nutritional factors and practical considerations such as price and availability.

The paper argues that ratings focused on one feature can leave consumers with an incomplete picture. A product’s performance in one area does not explain its trade-offs elsewhere. Macdonald presents the framework as a way to help people understand those competing considerations and the food systems their purchases support.

As the paper states, “Against deliberate corporate obfuscation and marketing myths, the goal of a holistic score is not to create a new label, but to foster a new literacy, one that empowers us to see the reality of the systems we are funding.

Ultimately, beyond the spreadsheets and scores, lies a fundamental reckoning: our protein choice is a referendum on the compassion we are willing to show to animals, to the planet, and to future generations.”

The research builds on Macdonald’s work at the Better Protein Institute, which examines how much protein people need for optimal health, how to shift consumer behavior effectively, and where investment could support future protein sources. By questioning common assumptions and starting from first principles, that work addresses some of the most frequently Googled questions about dietary protein.

Reference: “What are the best sources of protein? Introducing the BPI Score” by Chris Macdonald, 1 July 2026, Frontiers in Sustainable Food Systems. DOI: 10.3389/fsufs.2026.1677796

A Look At The Salaries Of US Navy Carrier Air Wing Commanders In 2026

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

A US Navy Carrier Air Wing Commander (CAG) in 2026 oversees roughly 2,400 personnel, dozens of aircraft, and one of the most complex operational organizations in modern military aviation, but how much does that responsibility actually pay? Following the 3.8% military pay raise implemented under the FY26 NDAA, a Navy Captain (O-6) serving as a CAG can earn well over $200,000 annually once housing allowances, Aviation Incentive Pay, and deployment-related compensation are included.

At a time when commercial airlines continue recruiting experienced military aviators with salaries that can exceed $500,000 per year, CAG compensation has become part of a much larger conversation about retention, naval aviation readiness, and the future of carrier air wings built around the Boeing F/A-18E/F Super Hornet Block III and Lockheed MartinF-35C Lightning II.

Taking into account 2026 DFAS pay tables, Military-Ranks.org compensation data, Glassdoor salary submissions, Navy personnel policies, and official US Navy organizational information, this article breaks down the major factors shaping a Carrier Air Wing Commander’s earnings in 2026. The analysis examines several key areas: base pay and allowances, aviation incentive compensation, how CAG salaries compare with other senior naval aviators and airline pilots, and the operational realities that justify one of the Navy’s most demanding leadership roles.

A Carrier Air Wing Commander holds the rank of Navy Captain, pay grade O-6. According to the Defense Finance and Accounting Service (DFAS) 2026 pay tables, O-6 base pay starts at $8,067.90 per month for officers with fewer than two years of service at that grade, but a CAG, by definition, is not a junior captain. Most officers who earn a carrier air wing command have accumulated over 20 years of service, placing them firmly at the upper end of the pay scale.

At 20+ years, O-6 monthly base pay reaches $12,638.40 per month, and at maximum longevity steps it climbs to $14,282.40 per month — though federal law caps O-6 base pay at $15,000.00 per month. That translates to an annual base salary of roughly $151,660 to $171,388 for a typical CAG, before a single dollar of allowances or special pay is added.

The 2026 figure already reflects a 3.8% pay raise that took effect on January 1, 2026, after President Donald Trumpsigned the National Defense Authorization Act for Fiscal Year 2026 (FY26 NDAA) into law on December 18, 2025. That raise, as noted by NavyCS, was mandated by U.S.C. Title 37 and applies uniformly across all active military branches.

For historical perspective, that same O-6 pay grade in 2020 topped out near $12,638 per month; The accumulated raises of the last six years have added well over $1,600 per month to a senior captain's baseline earnings. That upward trajectory has been consistent, partly driven by Congress responding to retention pressures as the commercial airline industry entered one of its largest hiring cycles in history.

Base pay tells only part of the story. Military compensation is deliberately structured as a package, with non-taxable allowances and incentive pay that can add tens of thousands of dollars annually. For a Carrier Air Wing Commander, those components typically include several distinct streams. According to Military-Ranks.org, when the most common allowances are included, total annual compensation for an O-6 Navy Captain in 2026 ranges from $131,113 to as much as $205,687.

The largest single add-on is the Basic Allowance for Housing (BAH), a non-taxable, location-dependent payment designed to offset the cost of off-base housing. The average BAH for an O-6 with dependents sits at $3,075.37 per month ($36,904 per year), though at high-cost duty stations like Naval Air Station North Island in San Diego, CA, or the Washington, D.C. area, that figure rises substantially above the national average. Alongside BAH, every commissioned officer also receives a Basic Allowance for Subsistence (BAS) to offset food costs: according to The Military Wallet, the 2026 officer BAS rate is $328.48 per month ($3,941 per year), non-taxable, and uniform regardless of rank or assignment.

Aviation Incentive Pay (AvIP) is a further pillar of aviator compensation: as documented in the Defense Finance and Accounting Service (DFAS), AvIP maxes out at $1,000 per month ($12,000 per year) for aviators with 14 or more years of aviation service, a threshold any Carrier Air Wing Commander has long cleared. Finally, when a CAG's air wing deploys to an active combat zone or operates in an area subject to hostile fire, an additional hazardous duty payment applies: that rate stands at up to $250 per month ($3,000 per year).

Cutting across all of these allowances is a tax advantage that civilian compensation packages simply cannot replicate: BAH and BAS are entirely exempt from federal income tax, meaningfully increasing real take-home value compared to a nominally equivalent civilian salary. As already explained by Simple Flying, compensation for senior naval aviators rises sharply at the command and flag-officer levels, reflecting both operational responsibility and years of specialized aviation service.

To understand why a CAG earns what they earn, the compensation must be weighed against the sheer scope of the role. The "CAG" title dates to World War II, when carrier aviation was organized into "Air Groups" rather than the modern "Air Wings." The formal designation changed in 1963, but the acronym stayed as a small, enduring tribute to the role's lineage.

In 2026, the CAG commands a carrier air wing that typically embeds several squadrons aboard a Nimitz- or Gerald R. Ford-class carrier, operating strike fighter jets, electronic warfare aircraft, early warning platforms, helicopters, and logistics aircraft in an integrated fashion. As reported by LegalClarity.org, "a CAG is a Navy Captain (pay grade O-6) who oversees roughly 2,400 personnel and dozens of aircraft spanning multiple squadron types, making it one of the most demanding leadership positions in naval aviation."

That means a CAG commands more personnel than many Army colonels and manages an air arm more complex than the entire air forces of several NATO nations. As we recently noted, the Super Hornet remains the backbone of current carrier air wings, with the Block III upgrade program ensuring it will remain central to CAG operations well into the 2040s, which also means today's CAG must be conversant in cutting-edge avionics, networked warfare, and the integration of fifth-generation aircraft like the F-35C Lightning II alongside legacy platforms.

According to Glassdoor's March 2026 data, based on 117 salary submissions from active and former O-6 Navy Captains, the estimated average total salary is $203,144 per year, with a range from roughly $159,193 (25th percentile) to $263,446 (75th percentile). These numbers, while self-reported and not official, align well with the computed figures derived from the DFAS pay tables plus standard allowances, and indicate that real-world total compensation for a CAG falls comfortably in the $200,000+ range.

Placing the CAG's salary in context requires a look at both one rank below and one rank above. An O-5 Commander, the rank that typically commands an individual fighter or strike squadron aboard the carrier, earns a base pay of roughly $9,929 per month ($119,148 per year) at the 10-year mark, rising to a maximum of $12,280 per month.

That makes the O-6 CAG's base pay approximately 15–20% higher in dollar terms, but the gap in responsibility is far wider: a CAG runs nine squadrons and 2,400 people, whereas an O-5 squadron commander runs a single unit of roughly 200 to 300 personnel. Above the CAG sits the Rear Admiral (O-7 and O-8) tier, where base pay is no longer capped at $15,000 per month but instead climbs toward the Level II Executive Schedule cap of $18,808.20 per month.

A Rear Admiral Lower Half (O-7) with a carrier strike group command earns between $12,803.70 and $18,458.10 per month in base pay alone, or roughly $153,600 to $221,500 annually, meaningfully above the CAG's ceiling. The trade-off is that the path from CAG to flag officer is intensely competitive: a very small fraction of O-6s earn their first star.

The commercial aviation comparison is, as always, illustrative. According to our previous analysis of USAF pilot salaries, senior airline captains flying major widebody international routes can earn over $550,000 annually in 2026, roughly three times the total compensation of a Navy O-6 with full allowances. The financial gap is simply not closable from a base pay perspective, which is precisely why Aviation Retention Bonuses (AvB) and quality-of-life factors remain critical levers in keeping experienced naval aviators in uniform through the CAG pipeline.

Discover how the growing experience gap is reshaping aviation worldwide.

Every salary figure cited in this article comes with an important context that can shift the real value of compensation significantly. The BAH figure, for example, is highly location-sensitive: an O-6 stationed at Naval Air Station Lemoore (in California's Central Valley) receives a different BAH rate than one based at NAS Oceana in Virginia Beach, VA, or attached to a forward-deployed unit in Japan.

Navy personnel assigned to overseas bases like NAF Atsugi or MCAS Iwakuni receive Overseas Housing Allowance (OHA) instead of BAH, calculated differently and potentially higher. On some overseas assignments, additional Cost of Living Allowances (COLA) can add several hundred dollars per month on top of that.

Deployment also changes the compensation calculus. When a carrier strike group deploys to a combat zone, as multiple carrier strike groups did across the Red Sea, the eastern Mediterranean, and the Indo-Pacific in recent years, service members may qualify for hostile fire pay ($225/month), tax exclusion on base pay earned in a combat zone (a benefit that, for an O-6, can represent a tax saving of $30,000 to $40,000 per year), and in some cases imminent danger pay.

Readers should also note that Aviation Bonus (AvB) figures are not included in the totals above. AvB contracts are negotiated at the end of a service obligation and are not universally offered or accepted at the CAG career point. While some O-6 aviators do receive retention bonuses, these are managed at the Navy Bureau of Personnel (BUPERS) level and vary by community need, timing, and individual agreements. A CAG who is within two to three years of potential retirement may or may not be eligible for or interested in a new AvB contract.

A Carrier Air Wing Commander in 2026 earns a base salary of approximately $151,660 to $171,388 per year. When standard allowances, Aviation Incentive Pay, and broader military benefits are included, total compensation typically rises to between $200,000 and $225,000 annually, with the possibility of climbing even higher during combat deployments.

These are numbers that would make any senior manager in the civilian industry pause, but they also reflect the extraordinary scale of the role itself. A CAG effectively commands the equivalent of a mid-sized air force from the flight deck of a 100,000-ton nuclear-powered aircraft carrier, overseeing roughly 2,400 personnel and dozens of aircraft while operating in some of the world’s most demanding and strategically sensitive environments.

The 3.8% military pay raise implemented on January 1, 2026, represents another attempt by Congress and the Pentagon to narrow the compensation gap between military aviation and the civilian airline industry. Even so, the difference remains significant. Senior airline captains flying the largest commercial widebody aircraft still routinely earn two to three times more than a CAG, a reality that continues to influence Navy retention efforts for highly experienced strike aviators. At the same time, the job itself is becoming increasingly complex.

Ongoing Block III Super Hornet upgrades, the continued integration of the F-35C, and the Navy’s evolving carrier air wing structure are making the CAG position more technically demanding than ever, even as commercial airlines continue competing aggressively for the same pool of elite aviators. The broader context of carrier aviation economics, including the Block III Super Hornet upgrades and the ongoing F-35C integration discussed across Simple Flying's coverage, makes the CAG billet more technically demanding than ever, even as the commercial pull on the talent pipeline remains intense.
