EMBT shelters Naples metro station beneath "natural and welcoming" vaulted timber roof

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Spanish architecture studio EMBT has completed the Naples Underground Central Station in Italy, contrasting its modernist surroundings with a wavy timber roof.

The station, which is shortlisted in the Infrastructure and transport category of Dezeen Awards 2026, sits within the Centro Direzionale, a business district in Naples designed in the 1970s by Japanese architect Kenzo Tange.

The 10,000-square-metre building was designed to replace a previously understated metro entrance with a new public plaza that felt more integrated with the surrounding city.

As EMBT founder Benedetta Tagliabue previously told Dezeen during the station’s construction, the curvilinear timber canopy was designed in direct response to what she called the “artificial” feel of the modernist Centro Direzionale.

“Kenzo Tange designed Centro Direzionale, and he built a very Kenzo Tange-type of city with reflective skyscrapers and a piazza, very 1970s, very clean,” she told Dezeen in 2021.

“It didn’t work because it’s so different from the rest of Naples,” Tagliabue continued. “So the idea of making a new station there and connecting with the new subway line was also to try to introduce a piece of nature and a piece of the original part of Naples into this artificial piece of the city.”

The roof of the station was constructed from arches and beams of glue-laminated (glulam) timber, which are partially supported by the existing concrete structure and shelter a floor finished in local Etna and Trani limestone.

According to Tagliabue, the branching columns and supports of these arches were designed to evoke the feeling of “walking through a forest”, while referencing the arched roofs of historic railway stations.

“We wanted the everyday experience of catching a train to feel more natural and welcoming, giving people a sense of being at home in the middle of the city,” she told Dezeen.

“We were also interested in what wood could bring to this particular neighbourhood. There is so much glass and concrete here; introducing timber was a way of bringing back a connection with nature, through its warmth and texture,” she added.

“The roof follows the geometry of the tracks, so something that is normally hidden underground becomes visible in the shape of the building.”

The vaulted canopy not only shelters the station’s ticket barriers and access routes, but extends towards the new surrounding paved plaza on either side, which faces a church to the north and incorporates new planting to the south.

Below ground, the station platforms are lined entirely with striped panels in bold shades of red, orange and yellow informed by Pompeian frescoes. This theme is continued above ground in the form of colourful silhouettes.

“Our station is not directly above archaeological remains, so we introduced our own references to that history, with a little humour. The reds and yellows recall Pompeii, and figures appear and disappear across the surfaces,” Tagliabue explained.

“I like the idea that a station can offer these unexpected encounters: along the way, you discover something about the city and feel more connected to it,” she added.

Elsewhere in Naples, sculptor Anish Kapoor and architecture studio AL_A previously designed two sculptural station entrances – one in weathered steel and the other in aluminium – as part of a project to connect the underground to the city’s Traiano district.

Other metro station architecture recently featured on Dezeen includes the mirrored Qasr AlHokm Metro Station in Saudi Arabia by Snøhetta and the series of buildings created for Melbourne’s Metro Tunnel by studios RSHP, Hassell and WW+P Architects.

Commanders look to stay alive against Giants in 'TNF' game Jayden Daniels called 'must win'

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Fueled by defeat, Washington Commanders quarterback Jayden Daniels was not shy about labeling his team's next game.

"Must win," Daniels told reporters after the Commanders' 20-13 loss to the Los Angeles Rams on Sunday.

That date with destiny has arrived 96 hours later, with his team facing the New York Giants on "Thursday Night Football" Nov. 12 (8:15 p.m. ET, Amazon Prime Video). But head coach Dan Quinn, facing job-security questions with his team sitting at 2-5-1 and heading into a mini-bye, does not see the game in the same light.

"For me … I try to avoid the declarations of that. I've been around the league long enough to know that lots of season to play and lots of football to go," Quinn said.

Not afraid to speak for everybody in the organization in this instance, Quinn offered a different way to categorize it.

"We need this and we're battling for it," Quinn said. "That's really where the focus is, but we don't get into the big declaration stuff."

During the Week 5 meeting between the two teams, it was the lack of an outcome – the two teams played the lone tie in the NFL this season – that seemed to doom the Commanders' season. Daniels and the offense mismanaged the clock with no timeouts remaining in the final minute of overtime, and the clock expired before the team could kick the game-winning field goal.

"We know that the Giants," Daniels said this week. "They have a lot of good athletic players out there that they like to get into the mix and blitz, you know, try to create mismatch and matchups and stuff like that. So, we just gotta prepare for that and then really adjust from there on out."

Now another prime-time matchup against a division rival presents an additional high-profile assignment in a season defined by bad optics. The following week, on "Monday Night Football" against the San Francisco 49ers, the Commanders once again appeared confused – this time about what down it was – when Daniels threw the ball away on fourth down. Quinn's reaction did not assuage the critics of his game management. Neither did the call on the final play of the Rams' loss when the ball did not reach the end zone despite trailing by a touchdown.

A win against the Giants would move Washington two games back in the loss column and keep the Commanders alive in the NFC East race, with the Giants using backup quarterback J.J. McCarthy, who enters the matchup nursing a hip pointer that limited him last Sunday against the Philadelphia Eagles.

Part of the Commanders' slow start is tied to Quinn's two first-year coordinators, David Blough (offense) and Daronte Jones (defense). Quinn shot down speculation that he could make staff changes with another loss. Then there is the question of Quinn's own job security, especially if the Commanders find themselves.

"I'd say we're all frustrated," Quinn said.

What encouraged Quinn during practice this week was the offense having zero incompletions prior to the game and executing during the situational periods – red zone and third down. Washington ranks 23rd in red-zone touchdown drives and last in third-down conversion rate. Luckily for the Commanders, the Giants are last in red-zone defense, allowing a touchdown on nearly four out of every five drives that reach their 20-yard line (78.95%).

"He's still growing as a coordinator, first-time coordinator, still going through his growing pains and stuff like that," Daniels said of Blough. "So, I just try to do the best I can, just go out there, execute, doesn't matter what the play call is, it doesn't matter anything. If we got all 11 people going in the same direction, we have a chance."

West Ham Taty Transfer Talks Could Open Door To Goalkeeper Deal

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

According to a number of reports, River Plate are exploring a move for Valentín Castellanos, with an initial loan proposal understood to be one of the options being discussed. The Argentine giants are believed to be assessing whether a temporary agreement could be structured with West Ham contributing towards a portion of the forward’s wages to help make the deal financially viable.

The Argentinian side are also open to exploring a deal in which they would acquire Castellanos while West Ham retain a percentage of his future transfer rights, reducing the upfront fee required.

While the prospect of regular football in South America may appeal to the player, any negotiations are unlikely to be straightforward.

A Taty transfer to River Plate could result in Beltran moving in the opposite direction.

While West Ham are understood to be open to parting ways with Castellanos, the club would want a significantly higher transfer fee than the figures currently being considered by River Plate, creating a sizeable gap between the two clubs’ positions.

Our trusted transfer source suggests it’s unlikely that the Argentinians can afford the striker, as teams over there don’t have any money, yet reaffirmed the striker is very much up for sale.

From West Ham’s perspective, agreeing to subsidise wages while also accepting a heavily discounted transfer fee makes little financial sense unless there are performance-related add-ons or future sell-on clauses that could bridge the gap.

The situation remains at an early stage and there is no indication that a breakthrough is imminent. However, River Plate’s interest appears genuine, and negotiations could continue if the Argentine club can move closer to West Ham’s expectations.

There are also suggestions that Santiago Beltrán, as reported by Gonzo yesterday, is of interest to West Ham, which opens up the possibility of the goalkeeper being included in any deal.

For now, the ball is firmly in River Plate’s court. If they want to bring Castellanos back to South American football, a significantly improved proposal will be required before West Ham consider doing business.

Scout Motors has 212,000 vehicle reservations as $2 billion South Carolina plant nears completion

📅 2026-11-12  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

BLYTHEWOOD, S.C. — Electric vehicle startup Scout Motors has notched roughly 212,000 reservations for its upcoming EVs as the company nears completion of its new $2 billion plant in South Carolina, CEO Scott Keogh told CNBC.

The reservations are for the Volkswagen-backed company's Terra pickup truck and Traveler SUV, which are expected to start full production next year before deliveries begin to customers in 2028.

Reservations do not necessarily translate to sales, as the U.S. automotive industry has learned during the past decade, especially with highly anticipated all-electric trucks such as the Ford F-150 Lightning, Tesla Cybertruck and others that garnered strong interest that didn't lead to meaningful numbers of purchases.

Keogh said he expects Scout could roughly double those reservations by the time customer production begins, but he said the more important thing is to convert early interest into actual sales as production nears.

"It's been a consistent drumbeat," Keogh said during an interview at Scout's plant site outside Columbia, South Carolina. "I think we could easily be up over 400,000 with reservations without doing too much effort. The big thing for us is how much of the initial production can we 100% presell."

Keogh said achieving an order book of 12-to-18 months "would be great" and something he thinks the company can do before production. Scout on Thursday said construction is "in its final phase."

Scout's vehicles will be offered as all-electric versions as well as electric hybrid variants, known as extended-range electric vehicles, or EREVs, that include an engine as a backup generator for the electric motors and batteries.

Scout, a former American vehicle brand from 1961 to 1980, was expected to exclusively offer EVs in a bid for the German automaker to expand its presence in the U.S. However, slower-than-expected adoption of EVs and higher costs led the company to decide to offer EREVs as well.

Keogh said the EREVs and SUV are dominating reservations thus far, with roughly 87% choosing EREV models and 75% being SUVs.

Given the interest signaled by reservations, Keogh said the company is expected to launch with the EREVs first, followed by the EVs. He also said the company plans to launch with mid-level models first, a deviation from many companies that begin with their highest-priced models.

"The No.1 thing for me is I want to go get the best volume as soon as possible," Keogh said. "In my mind, it's how quick can you get to 100,000 units? It's at 100,000 units where you can start to punch through."

His comments come months after Rivian Automotive received criticism online for launching its R2 SUV with its highest-priced models first, despite that being a common practice across the automotive industry.

Keogh said Scout's South Carolina plant is about 85% complete, but supporting infrastructure and a $300 million supplier park continue to be installed.

Reservations for Scout's vehicles initially opened in October 2024, when it revealed the Traveler and Terra, which feature sleek, yet rugged, exterior appearances that pay some homage to the brand's historic models.

Elizabeth Warren’s Plan to Abolish Credit Cards

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer (D., N.Y.), and Sen. Elizabeth Warren (D., Mass.) host a roundtable on housing affordability and accessibility on Capitol Hill in Washington, D.C., January 7, 2026.

Elizabeth Warren, never short on annoyances, thinks that interest on consumer credit cards is too high.

The average rate currently hovers around 21 percent. Her solution: Pass a law to cap interest rates at 10 percent. She says she has a bill ready to go, and like-minded lawmakers have signaled their support for controlling rates.

Like any other price ceiling, an interest cap would create a shortage of the product it attempts to make more affordable. With only a tiny fraction of credit cards now offering interest at 10 percent or less, capping rates at that level would force lenders to dramatically restrict accounts — requiring borrowers to put up collateral or limiting access to people with exceptionally good credit — or eliminate them outright.

To progressives, interest on credit cards is nothing but usury — the exploitation of poor borrowers who can’t afford to pay their bills on time. Warren’s sales pitch implies that, under her bill, people would enjoy the same credit just at a lower cost. In reality, almost everyone would see their credit cards disappear.

Regrettably, Warren may also find allies for her proposal among Republicans. She claims she is open to working with President Trump, who called in 2024 for a temporary cap on credit card interest of “around 10 percent.” Senator Josh Hawley (R., Mo.), a right-wing progressive of sorts, recently joined hands with Bernie Sanders to translate that thoughtless musing into legislation.

Interest rates are not some arbitrary number. They are the price for lenders to provide liquidity on the promise, but never the certainty, that it will be repaid. To issue loans through a credit card, banks must first obtain the money by borrowing it themselves, either from depositors or other financial institutions. Interest must be paid on that debt and passed through to cardholders.

It’s true that rates on credit cards, as well as other consumer loans, are typically much higher than those for mortgages and businesses. That is because personal credit is one of the riskiest kinds to extend. Unlike secured loans, repayment depends wholly on borrowers’ creditworthiness. That often fails to hold up: 13 percent of credit-card balances are seriously past due, and approximately 5 percent of debt is never repaid. Higher interest rates exist to compensate for these losses.

Rates also vary based on individual creditworthiness. Borrowers with poor credit history, or people just starting to build their history after leaving childhood or moving to the country, have the most to lose from interest caps. They must accept costlier rates to obtain any credit whatsoever, and if lenders can’t adjust for their added risk, they will simply be cut off. Those in need of funds will instead have to turn to pawnshops or illegal loan sharks.

The credit card industry could always use more competition to keep interest rates at their fair market level. Ironically, the Dodd-Frank Act that Elizabeth Warren championed likely reduced the availability of consumer credit by increasing lenders’ regulatory burden. Warren also despises credit reporting agencies for tracking borrowers’ repayment histories, even though they replaced a far more discretionary and intrusive system in which bankers evaluated applicants’ personal reputations. For someone so appalled by alleged discrimination in finance, she should love objective credit reports.

By and large, Americans like their credit cards and wish to keep them. In trying to rescue consumers from their own financial decisions, Warren would deprive them of their plastic and consign them to infinitely more treacherous alternatives.

More than two years have passed since Biogen announced results from a Phase 3 study in lupus. The drug was dapirolizumab and the outcome was positive, in case you were wondering. In the first half of December, Biogen will hope for a repeat, and once again, it’s another lupus drug, litifilimab, that is being evaluated in twin Phase 3 studies.

The looming readout from the TOPAZ-1 and TOPAZ-2 studies is an important test of the R&D revamp and growth strategy that Biogen CEO Chris Viehbacher implemented when he joined the company in 2022. It’s also risky.

Man United Supporters’ Trust underlines ‘depressing moment’ after Michael Carrick sacked

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

The supporters’ group said “responsibility for United’s problems... lie with the ownership” rather than Carrick

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

The Manchester United Supporters’ Trust (MUST) has branded Michael Carrick’s sacking “another depressing moment” and argued the club’s problems rest primarily with its ownership, in a now-familiar plea to end the ongoing feeling of crisis at Old Trafford.

United sacked Carrick just 10 games into the Premier League season with the club in the relegation zone and having won just once in this domestic campaign.

The club’s hierarchy met minority owner Jim Ratcliffe in Monaco on Wednesday to discuss Carrick’s future and a statement on Thursday said that it had “come to the extremely difficult conclusion that a change is necessary in order to achieve our ambitions”.

Former Southampton, Tottenham and Chelsea manager Mauricio Pochettino, who currently manages the US men’s national team, is in line to replace him.

The club statement paid tribute to former midfielder Carrick as a “Manchester United legend” and the MUST statement concurred, saying “his standing among supporters remains undiminished”, but criticised the ownership for the spiral the club has found itself in.

It said: “Michael Carrick’s departure marks another depressing moment for Manchester United. We thank him for his service and for restoring hope during the second half of last season. His standing among supporters remains undiminished.

“The reason Manchester United fans have continued to support Michael Carrick in recent weeks is that they know that responsibility for United’s problems do not primarily rest with him or indeed his predecessors as manager. They lie with the ownership.

“The Glazers’ debt-funded takeover continues to burden the club. When Ineos came in they promised sporting excellence, but yet with repeated changes in football leadership we have seen anything but.

“It is time for the owners to explain themselves and show some accountability.”

MUST continued to criticise “aggressive ticketing policies” which “have further damaged relations with supporters”, arguing “supporters are being asked to pay the price for the owners’ mismanagement as well as their stadium ambitions through higher charges and diminished rights”.

It continued: “The unprecedented scale of the booing at Old Trafford, alongside public criticism from senior players in recent weeks, now points to a deeper deterioration. These are signs of a club becoming more divided, with the bond between supporters, players and those running Manchester United under increasing strain.”

Defender Lisandro Martinez criticised the club’s hierarchy for not doing more to support Carrick in the transfer market following the 2-1 Champions League defeat to Atletico Madrid last month, while dire on-pitch performances have resulted in protests against the ownership and loud booing of the players, although there were public displays of support for Carrick by fans at Old Trafford.

MUST concluded: “The owners must allow senior club management to change direction: show accountability, rebuild trust, listen to supporters and recognise that their loyalty is fundamental to football success.

“Another change of head coach cannot repair what the ownership continues to undermine.”

Ariana Grande Brings Her Two Great Loves—Ricky Álvarez and Rare Vintage—to the Red Carpet

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Ariana Grande brought two of her greatest loves to the red carpet last night: boyfriend Ricky Álvarez and a very, very good piece of vintage.

At the New York premiere of Focker-in-Law, the singer and actor made her first red carpet appearance with Álvarez since rekindling their romance earlier this year. And if their constant embraces, adoring glances, and kisses were anything to go by, the pair are very much still in the honeymoon phase.

Stylist Law Roach selected a vintage 1990s Badgley Mischka gown for the occasion. The delicate, spaghetti-strapped design featured a pale silver-gray windowpane check, a fitted bodice scattered with tiny beaded flowers, and a voluminous floor-skimming skirt. She accessorized with Swarovski Matrix hoop earrings—a recent favorite—and traded the flipped bob she’s been sporting of late for a sleek micro ponytail, tied with a little ivory ribbon. Makeup was also her go-to style of glowy and highlight-dominant.

Roach is on a particularly impressive archival streak of late. He’s been mining the fashion vaults for Kim Kardashian’s All's Fair press tour, pulling everything from 1990s Dolce & Gabbana to Paco Rabanne couture. Just last weekend, he dressed Anya Taylor-Joy in a spectacularly ruffled spring 1997 Pierre Cardin ensemble for the LACMA Art+Film Gala.

Grande has long been a devotee of fashion’s more whimsical, romantic history. Her love of vintage reached particularly fanciful heights during the Wicked press tours, but the singer has since held firmly to her soft, ultra-feminine aesthetic with 1950s silhouettes and diaphanous fabrics. At one point, her dedication to the look was so pronounced that some wondered whether she was quietly auditioning for a rumored Audrey Hepburn biopic. Roach revealed that he’d bought the Badgley Mischka gown months earlier, drawn to its connection with the era of the original Meet the Parents film.

Álvarez, for his part, kept things considerably more low-key. The dancer and photographer wore a relaxed black blazer over an open-neck white shirt, tucked into dark indigo jeans with rolled cuffs, a leather belt, and brown shoes. Some classic easy-going boyfriend dressing. He spent much of the evening with an arm around Grande’s waist, while she leaned into him, held his hands, and beamed up at him for the cameras.

Of course, the couple’s love story has its own history. Grande and Álvarez first met when he worked as a backup dancer on her Honeymoon Tour in 2015. They dated for around a year before splitting in 2016, and he later received a memorable name-check in her 2018 breakup anthem, “thank u, next.” Almost a decade later, however, things have come full circle. Rumors of a reunion began circulating this summer when the pair were spotted together in Austin, Texas, during Grande’s Eternal Sunshine tour, before more sightings followed in Florida and New York. By August, they were Instagram official, and Grande had debuted a new tattoo reading “I do love you”, seemingly matching a similar inscription on Álvarez’s collarbone. The two were also spotted arm-in-arm around London, where Grande completed her 10-night residency at the O2. She even began rewriting the Ricky lyrics in “thank u, next” onstage. At one Brooklyn show, she sang, “Wrote some songs about Ricky / We always find our way back.” By the tour’s September finale in London, the sentiment had become considerably more definitive: “I’m tryna make him my last.”

More recently, they’ve been enjoying some more ordinary couple activities, including cheering on runners at the New York City Marathon earlier this month. Grande has also enlisted Álvarez for a particularly kiss-heavy R.E.M. Beauty lip-product guessing game on Instagram. The adoration and affection is showing no signs of slowing down.

Between a decade-old romance finding new life and a beautiful dress pulled from the archives, Ariana Grande is making a persuasive case for revisiting the past.

Deadline revealed nearly two years ago, before CIISA was up and running properly, that Nandy was considering legislation that would force UK broadcasters to regularly fund the body, which emerged from the Noel Clarke scandal and Time’s Up UK movement.

The whistleblowing service opened a few weeks ago in earnest but regular donations are required to keep it running, especially if there is an influx of complaints following these high-profile allegations. The body would also like to expand its scope at some point to areas like arbitration. CIISA representatives have been invited to Nandy’s meeting and we understand CIISA will host an event in parliament later this month.

CIISA has indicated that running costs would be around £1.5M ($2M) in its first year, rising to just more than £2M the following one to fund its small team and operations. The body has floated a funding model based on organizations paying an ongoing yearly contribution of a maximum 0.1% of their annual turnover. Current financial backers include the likes of the BBC, ITV, Sky and big production groups.

Nandy and other senior government ministers are also focused on non-disclosure agreements, with Bridget Phillipson, the UK’s womens and equalities minister, telling Sky News such agreements should not be “abused in order to silence women.”

All this has contributed to a feeling within the industry that enough is enough.

An open letter has been doing the rounds, signed by dozens of actors, writers and directors, which is titled “Let’s make this a turning point” and urges industry action.

“We call on every union, every company and every industry organisation to stop enabling a systemic cycle of abuse, whether through unconscious inaction or deliberate distraction,” it reads. “Review your processes. Protect those who speak out. Become less offended by your inaction being named than you are by the actions you are being asked to recognise.”

Brand was sentenced earlier this week to 10-and-a-half years in prison for rape and sexual assault and the TV industry has been heavily implicated, with one of the women he sexually assaulted working for Channel 4 at the time. Earlier this week, The Guardian and ITV News published allegations against of sexually exploitative activity against women and girls from Walliams, allegations that he denies. Meanwhile, Channel 4 axed Married at First Sight last week after rape allegations.

This new adapter was first announced way back in March 2026, bringing with it some welcome upgrades to the hardware including:

You’ll also get both USB-C and USB-A cables in the box with the adapter, and it’s reiterated that the thing that makes this wireless Android Auto adapter stand out is that it’s the “only one engineered by Google.”

Motorola MA2’s price was originally said to be $39.95, but the final price is $69.99 on Amazon. That’s for the US, at least, as some users in other regions have been able to purchase the device for weeks, even months.

Toyota and Subaru hit the ‘sweet spot’ with affordable electric SUVs

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Toyota and Subaru’s new electric SUVs are catching on with buyers, offering various options and prices ranging from about $35,000 to $45,000, or the “sweet spot” of the US market.

The US electric vehicle market rebounded in the third quarter with 262,649 EVs sold, according to Kelley Blue Book estimates, up 6.1% from Q2 and the strongest performance of the year.

Although nearly every automaker has sold fewer EVs so far this year than in the same period in 2025, as buyers rushed to claim the $7,500 tax credit before it expired, a handful of brands are seeing considerably higher demand.

Outside of Rivian, Toyota and Subaru are the only brands that have higher year-over-year EV sales in the US through the third quarter.

Combined, Toyota and Subaru have sold nearly 50,000 EVs through the first nine months of 2026, more than double compared to the same period last year.

Toyota alone has sold nearly 35,000 electric vehicles, up 183.4% YOY, while Subaru sold 14,630, up 46.7%. And that’s not even including the additional 12,676 sold under the Lexus brand, up 137.4% YOY.

While the two Japanese automakers were infamously slow to transition to battery electric vehicles, the timing worked in their favor.

After launching the heavily updated bZ in late 2025, previously called the bZ4X, Toyota’s electric SUV suddenly became one of America’s most popular EVs. Through Q3, it was the fourth best-selling EV in the US, trailing only the Hyundai IONIQ 5, Tesla Model 3, and Tesla Model Y.

Starting at $35,000, the Toyota bZ is one of the most affordable EVs in the US. It also offers up to 314 miles of range in the FWD Plus trim, which starts at $37,980.

Toyota and Subaru now each sell three electric SUVs, which share the same platform and technology.

With prices ranging from roughly $35,000 to about $45,000, Stephanie Valdez Streaty, director of industry insights at Cox Automotive, said (via Bloomberg) Toyota and Subaru’s new EVs are affordable and hit the “sweet spot” of the US car market.

Toyota also sells the smaller, sporty C-HR, starting at $37,080, and the larger, more rugged bZ Woodland, which starts at $45,380.

Subaru, on the other hand, sells the updated Solterra ($38,495, plus the smaller Uncharted ($34,995) and midsize Trailseeker ($39,995).

Karla Fisher, a Marine and retired engineer, told Bloomberg that the Subaru Trailseeker was her “most favorite car of all time.”

While she considered buying an EV before the $7,500 federal credit expired last September, none met her requirements at the time. Fisher wanted at least 7″ of ground clearance, 250 miles or more range, and a price that was similar, or even less than a comparible gas-powered vehicle.

The Subaru Trailseeker offers all of that and starts at under $40,000. Toyota and Subaru’s electric SUVs all start at about $45,000 or less, which is even less than the average new vehicle.

According to the latest Kelley Blue Book figures, the average price for a new vehicle in the US reached a record $51,109 in October, up 2.1% YOY.

Subaru spokesperson Dominick Infante said the affordable EV prices are mainly thanks to its partnership with Toyota. “We do believe there’s an EV future,” Infante added.

Next year, both brands will add a three-row electric SUV with the Toyota Highlander EV and Subaru Getaway.

Thinking about making the switch to an EV? With the 2027 models at dealerships, now could be a good time to find a deal on current 2026 models. You can use the links below to see what’s available at Toyota and Subaru dealerships near you.

Pompliano Says Bitcoin Spot ETFs Are Safer and More Convenient Than Hardware Wallets for Bitcoiners Holding Over $10,000

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Pompliano’s comments on Bitcoin hardware wallets and Bitcoin ETFs come at a time when more people show interest in BTC amid security threats.

Bitcoin expert Anthony Pompliano today tackled a crucial question that serious Bitcoin investors often ask. The question is: between hardware wallets and ETFs, which one offers the best outcome for securing and getting exposure to Bitcoin?

In an interview released early today, the celebrated crypto analyst answered questions savvy investors want answered about Bitcoin self-custody versus Bitcoin ETFs.

Today, Bitcoin adoption grows at a remarkable rate. As more investors seek to invest in the cryptocurrency, there are two major ways to do so: gaining exposure through Bitcoin hardware wallets (self-custody) or Bitcoin ETFs.

In the podcast, Pompliano admitted that using spot ETFs is more secure than storing Bitcoin in a hardware wallet. According to the crypto expert, spot ETFs offer an accessible approach, providing investors with a simplified, regulated way to buy and invest in Bitcoin through their existing brokerage accounts. As Pompliano stated, ETFs stand out because they emphasize the importance of regulation in providing investor protection and market integrity.

Pompliano further recognized spot ETFs as the safest and the most convenient option for investors holding between $10,000 and $10 million worth of BTC. He said these investment vehicles offer greater liquidity than directly holding BTC on hardware wallets. They provide investors with diversified exposure to stock exchanges and on-chain markets.

However, he said convenience comes with caveats, as ETFs provide indirect exposure to Bitcoin. This means that investors don’t actually own the underlying Bitcoin. Also, ETFs might not provide investors with the same level of control as directly owning BTC in self-custody wallets.

This highlights the advantages of Bitcoin hardware wallets, which allow customers to hold BTC directly, giving them greater control. However, this level of control comes with the responsibility of managing private keys, asset control, and security risks.

The debate between self-custody and ETFs comes at a time when the crypto industry continues to experience increasing security breaches, with the latest and largest one being the Binance infiltration.

Such unfortunate hacking events compel customers to rethink how to secure their BTC while still accessing strong liquidity. The self-custody approach offers direct ownership and control of BTC, though it requires technical know-how and involves greater security risks. On the other hand, ETFs offer a simple, regulated way to gain exposure to the asset, with diversified liquidity. They, however, come with some limitations.

Art Week NYC Kicks off at Metropolitan Museum of Art with New York Pols and Art Leaders

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Dozens of leaders from the New York art and political worlds rubbed shoulders at the Metropolitan Museum of Art on Tuesday morning at the launch of Art Week NYC, a citywide gathering celebrating New York’s leading galleries, institutions, and cultural spaces, organized by ARTnews, Art in America, and Artforum. The inaugural event takes place from November 11–14, 2026.

The distinguished roster of speakers included Julie Menin, City Council Speaker; City Council Deputy Speaker Nantasha Williams; Manhattan Borough President Brad Hoylman-Sigal; New York State Senator Jose Serrano; and City Council member Virginia Maloney. Related Articles After Galliano Firestorm, Met Plans New Costume Institute Exhibition Unlikely to Offend Anyone The Met Museum and Vacheron Constantin Unveil Artworks Produced Through New Residency

In attendance were VIPs like advisor Augusto Arbizo; MoMA PS1 director Connie Butler; Lisson Gallery international director Alex Logsdail; Arts Council England chairman Nicholas Serota; and Simone Subal, senior director at Paula Cooper Gallery, along with ARTnews/Art in America editor Sarah Douglas and publisher Erica Lubow Necarsulmer as well as Artforum co-editor Rachel Wetzler and publisher Danielle McConnell.

The gathering took place in the light-filled Charles Engelhard Court, anchored by the Neoclassical facade of the Second Branch Bank of the United States (1822–24) and featuring sculptures by American artists such as Daniel Chester French, Hiram Powers, and Augustus Saint-Gaudens.

All were there, said David Breslin, the museum’s curator in charge of the modern and contemporary art department, “to launch what we know will become a defining tradition in our city’s cultural landscape—and that’s saying a lot.”

Kicking off the remarks, City Council Speaker Julie Menin looked to history to set the tone, noting that it was 100 years ago when pioneering New York dealer Edith Halpert launched what would become the Downtown Gallery, which was early to show American modern art and folk art. To signal its spirit of inclusivity, Menin said, it was initially called Our Gallery, adding, “As the very first citywide contemporary art festival, that is precisely the same spirit that Art Week NYC has built here.” The City Council was proud to allocate a record $323 million in its latest budget to the Department of Cultural Affairs, she added.

Manhattan Borough President Brad Hoylman-Sigal recalled bringing together all the city’s local community boards at the museum recently and finding that some had never before visited. “Art Week is a way to invite New Yorkers—just like we invited those community board members to the Met to experience art—to meet New Yorkers, as they say, where they are,” he said, and called New York’s hundreds of galleries, many of them participating in Art Week, “magnificent little crown jewels.”

Citing Public Theater founder Joseph Papp to the effect that art belongs to everyone, Hoylman-Sigal noted, “I’m very proud to have committed 100 percent of our capital dollars to arts and cultural institutions.” That was $50 million in discretionary funds, he noted, adding that the recipient institutions were called upon to leverage the public dollars to raise money from private donors, and the 83 cultural institutions raised an additional $52 million through what he called the “Manhattan Multiplier.”

Wrapping up the event, City Council member Virginia Maloney noted that her namesake grandmother was an artist before it was common for women to have careers. Her brother-in-law is also an artist, she noted, adding, “They both taught me that art changes the way that you view the world. As chair of the Economic Development Committee, I also know that culture and art is not a luxury in New York. It’s an economic engine for tourism, for visitors, for employers, and for businesses that surround the organizations that we’re supporting today. When we invest in the arts, we know that we recoup the benefits tenfold.”

For a full listing of member galleries and institutions and a calendar of events, head to Art Week NYC.

Innovators at Work: The Price for Breaking the Rules

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 11, 2026 | Reviewed by Davia Sills

Ethical disruptive innovators identify and solve problems through prosocial and creative means that often unsettle group norms.

Disruption can be interpreted both as destructive and divine, depending on the professional culture. For example, part-time Sherwin-Williams employee Tony Piloseno, a senior at the time at Ohio University, used his popular TikTok channel to share his paint-mixing acumen, a clever contribution meant to bolster the company’s products, yet instead of accolades, he was served termination papers for violating company policy. However, instead of accepting one of the multiple job offers spurred by the news coverage, he poured his energy into starting his own paint company, a bold innovator’s move (Tonester Paints, n.d.).

Organizational norms govern how employees typically behave at work, but norms can be messy business, for institutions possess multiple forms. There are injunctive norms, which are the “ought to” behaviors, and descriptive norms, which detail what people actually do. When a gap exists between the two, the Focus Theory of Normative Conduct suggests we follow the norms we see others live out (Cialdini et al., 1991).

For example, let’s say that during faculty meetings, university administrators regularly profess that they value employee well-being (injunctive norms), yet faculty workload dictates 70-hour work weeks, and the university gym is free to students, but not to employees. Faculty will work longer hours and exercise fewer hours, putting their health at risk, because the descriptive norm is what plays out on campus despite injunctive norms to the contrary (Cialdini et al., 1990; Roswag et al., 2026; Skogstad et al., 2017).

We are shaped by those with whom we share our social spaces, so what happens when an employee decides to think past tradition, explore new ideas, and forge unique connections in a quest to innovate novel solutions to organizational problems (Lawande, 2023)?

Dyer and colleagues (2011) studied just that, interviewing creators of groundbreaking products and founders and leaders of visionary companies. They shared their insights in the book The Innovator’s DNA: Mastering the Five Skills of Disruptive Innovators. Across their interviews, surveys, and research, five characteristics bubbled up from their data, finding that innovators passionately practice:

Another intriguing finding was that, though many companies’ leaders facilitated processes that encouraged innovation, they themselves did not engage in innovative work. Conversely, in highly innovative organizations, making up just 15 percent of Dyer et al.’s (2009) research, top executives were among the ones actually doing, not delegating, innovation. Therefore, the injunctive (ought to) and descriptive (actually doing) organizational norms were in alignment, thus providing a clear pathway for others to join in or initiate creative work.

Again, we tend to emulate what we see, and leaders set that agenda. But what happens to innovative employees in organizations where leadership views their innovator’s DNA as a threat, not an asset?

Ethical disruption awakens innovation, but as Tony experienced, shaking trees has its costs; keepers of the status quo sometimes go to considerable lengths to neutralize norm-breakers. Norm violations are categorized as either constructive, such as crafting innovative products that surpass current offerings, or destructive, like purposefully denigrating a colleague’s reputation. The disruptor’s intentions and the innovation’s success also factor into how the norm is classified (Bennett et al., 2024). Though organizations often profess a desire for “out-of-the-box” thinkers, many nonconformists face roadblocks and repercussions. Whether the disruptor receives praise or pushback is, in large part, reflective of leadership.

According to McGregor’s (1960) seminal book, The Human Side of Enterprise, leaders adopt one of two theories when developing their style of management. Leaders with a Theory X view of management assume employees are lazy and shirk responsibility; therefore, they must be micromanaged, leaving little room for innovation. In contrast, leaders who view management through a Theory Y framework believe work can be fun, employees are internally motivated to do a good job, and that when given autonomy and resources, they can and will engage in creative work. Thus, under Theory Y leadership, innovators innovate.

Another way to think about leadership approaches that stymie innovation is through what researchers call control appraisal. Leaders with a low control appraisal struggle with self-doubt and believe they have limited ability to control outcomes. This group is unsettled by innovators, viewing them as unpredictable and destabilizing. Thus, they are quick to shut them down, often using withdrawal of opportunities and ostracization as containment strategies. By contrast, leaders with high control appraisal are self-confident and possess a strong sense of personal agency, or trust that they have the skills and fortitude to impact outcomes. These leaders are intrigued by innovators and work to form genuine connections, believing disruptors’ creativity is an asset, thus supporting and celebrating their work (Peng et al., 2026).

Social comparison theory is another helpful tool for considering leaders’ level of support for ethical disruptive innovators. It posits that leaders with a low core self-evaluation, or a propensity to question their ability to steer the ship, often become jealous of employees who exhibit high leadership potential (HLP), worried their star might surpass their own, and thus attempt to eliminate the competition by withdrawing opportunities. Conversely, comparison is minimal to nonexistent in leaders with a high core self-evaluation, and this positioning calls them to support innovative projects and proposals with the potential to bolster the institution’s success and more effectively serve communities (Yu et al., 2025).

Lastly, when faced with ethically disruptive innovators who don’t conform to the prevailing work culture, knowingly or unknowingly breaking group norms, leaders tend to react in one of five ways: forgive, reward, punish, ignore, or manipulate. According to research, however, only the reward reaction ensures the creative continues to engage in innovative behaviors. So, when a leader shuts down an innovator, the employee and the organization both lose out (Lin et al., 2016).

So, what does this all tell us about the types of leaders innovators need to engage in trailblazing work? First, innovators need leaders who clearly articulate and live into their mission, ensuring their injunctive norms (ought tos) and descriptive norms (what people actually do) are in alignment. Second, innovators benefit from leaders who build authentic relationships with their employees, not just rule-followers in their inner circle. Third, innovators thrive when working for leaders who personally engage in innovative work, and by that example, the institution develops a collective culture of creativity and care.

How <i><em>Marty Supreme</em></i> Costume Designer Miyako Bellizzi Really Shops

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

The consummate downtown girl joins Bazaar’s podcast to talk about collecting vintage skirt suits and Supreme hoodies

The Good Buy is a podcast from Harper’s Bazaar in which editors Leah Chernikoff and Lynette Nylander invite celebrities, designers, models, and tastemakers to talk shop: what they buy, where they got it, and why it matters. Come down the fashion rabbit hole and take a peek inside the closets and shopping carts of the world’s most stylish people. Learn how they use style to tell their stories.

From Uncut Gems to Marty Supreme, Oscar-nominated costume designer Miyako Bellizzi is responsible for dressing some of the most memorable films of the past decade. A longtime collaborator with the Safdie brothers—Joshua and Benjamin—she’s brought the filmmakers’ stories to life with detail-oriented styling that feels lived-in and real. Whether capturing the ostentatious wardrobe of a 2012 Diamond District hustler or crafting 1950s-accurate ensembles for over 3,000 background actors in the Timothée Chalamet–led ping-pong drama, she’s proven time and time again she has a knack for storytelling.

In her personal life, this expertise has translated into an eclectic closet full of vintage finds. Known for mixing high-end designer pieces with streetwear pulls and 1940s workwear, she dresses herself with as much intention as she does the characters you see on screen. Showing up to film this week’s installment of Harper’s Bazaar’s The Good Buy, she paired a fitted blazer from her collection of 1940s and ’50s skirt suits with shorts she made herself, Phoebe Philo flats, and sculptural silver statement earrings.

“I love costume design, I love fashion, I love film, I love it all,” she said. “I love people, so I think that’s why.”

In the episode, Bellizzi dives into everything from costuming the screen to shopping for herself. Read on for highlights and watch the show in full above.

“I’ve always loved vintage, I think my parents put me onto it. My grandma had all these clothes from New York from the ’50s and ’60s that she didn’t want, and my dad is a big vintage buyer. Even my Barbies were vintage Barbies. But I also just appreciate well-made pieces, I appreciate good fabrics, things that you can’t find today. As a designer, it’s fascinating to see how things were made back in different eras.

“I collect vintage 1940s and ’50s skirt suits. It’s always been my favorite era, which was why Marty Supreme was a dream film for me. I think I started collecting in high school—we used to have vintage fairs in San Francisco, where I’m from. I like the shape and silhouette. This is what Mugler referenced. I’m a historical person and I love the shapes and silhouettes of Mugler, and I have a lot of Mugler, but also I know where it comes from, so I like to go to the source.”

“I don’t personally buy a lot of contemporary brands for myself, other than Phoebe Philo and the Row. I [shop there for] very classic basics, because I find all the freaky weird things vintage.”

“When I was a kid, I was like 5’9” in the fifth grade. I was really tall. And we’d always have to shop in the boy’s section. When I first started, I was at Details Magazine. It was my first internship; that’s why I moved to New York. But I always felt out of place. I never felt like I fit in with the fashion girlies of that time. And so I remember enjoying menswear and learning about menswear and tailoring, and I found that was more inspiring to me than being in the women’s fashion closet.”

“I was at Vice and I was doing all the fashion stories, and there was a mass exodus and it was the group that left that asked if I would be interested in doing a film. I had never done one before, and I was like, ‘Sure, I’ll try.’ Then, [with the Safdie brothers,] we had mutual friends. The film industry is very small. Everything is timing. I never thought I wanted to be a costume designer; this was not the goal when I first moved to New York. But I love it because it’s this family—you travel with your crew and you’re doing these jobs together and you live together. You’re living and breathing this alternate reality. All you’re thinking about are these characters. It’s almost like how an actor has to get into character. You have to become the character you’re creating.”

“It was tough, honestly. You have to think about yourself being presented in that way. Traditionally, most costume designers aren’t fashion girlies. I was excited for it but it was also very stressful. I remember I was working in Paris at the time and I was staying with [designer] Gabe [Semeraro]. I was freaking out. I went to a few vintage archives there, being like, ‘At least I’m in Paris. I can find a dress.’ And I did find a really beautiful red Galliano dress that I loved that I was going to rent, but I was like, ‘I don’t know, it’s so expensive.’ One night, I was home with Gabe, and he was like, ‘Babe, I’m just gonna make one for you.’ It was one of my favorite dresses I’ve ever worn or seen.”

“Every time I wrap a film, it feels like an achievement. I remember I was doing a film in Hawaii, and when I was there, I loved that the women wore their wealth on their wrists. They stack solid gold bangles [on their wrists], and most of times they have names of people in their families [engraved]. And they’re the most beautiful things. I figured out where to find them—at a flea market—and I got one and had it engraved. Now I’ve turned it into a thing where I buy myself a piece of jewelry or something really nice after I wrap a film."

“I am very thoughtful about what I buy for myself. I never want to spend too much money on myself. But I have made a folder on my phone of pieces I regret not buying. We were in Paris and went to Alaïa and I wanted this pink set that I didn’t buy and I still think about it.”

“Now that we are approaching fall, I live in a long sleeve. That’s my Bay area [upbringing] coming out of me. I need to have a XXXL long-sleeve T-shirt, heavy cotton. That is my comfort layer. I bring one everywhere. That and a hoodie. Those are my two hometown pieces. And when I get obsessed with something, I find a lot of them. I have a lot of Supreme hoodies, although my new favorites are 6397, because they have fun colors.”

“I was thinking about what I would buy if I had all the money in the world. It’s a pair of cuffs—or, actually, anything from this jewelry designer Art Smith. It’s kind of modernist jewelry from a different era, like 50 years ago. So something like that. Sophie Buhai is kind of the modern-day equivalent. Pieces that could be in museums.”

“I’m going to be realistic—I did buy four pairs of shoes this week: Chanel flats [from Matthieu Blazy’s first collection] on the RealReal, a pair of [Michael Rider’s] Celine jazz shoes, two pairs of Bottega woven flats.”

How Michael Carrick's Man United fell apart: Players' bust-up on flight back from Madrid, weakness in training and on touchline - and why dressing-room sources believe divided board 'hung him out to dry'

📅 2026-11-11  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

By CHRIS WHEELER, NORTHERN SPORTS WRITER and NATHAN SALT - FOOTBALL REPORTER

Published: 10:30 EST, 11 November 2026 | Updated: 06:44 EST, 12 November 2026

For the last two days, Michael Carrick has followed his familiar route towards Manchester United’s training base, nudging his black Land Rover past the security barrier at Carrington before 8am, and making his way into work just as he has for much of the last two decades as a player, coach and head coach at the club.

While Carrick's fate has been decided by men in suits in Monaco and Manchester, he has put on his training gear and gone through the motions of coaching whichever players are left during the international break.

It has become increasingly apparent that Carrick was a dead man walking in the two weeks since the United board gave him a stay of execution and bought themselves time after the 3-0 defeat by Bournemouth last month.

Having effectively been brought in as a caretaker when United plucked him off the beach in Barbados in mid-January, he left much the same way: merely keeping the seat warm for Mauricio Pochettino in the chaotic days that have led to his sacking.

Sources close to the United dressing room believe Carrick has been ‘hung out to dry’ as United dithered over his future and then began a futile attempt to appoint Pochettino as his replacement in secret.

This is the story of how Carrick's United fell apart. It is the story of how disgruntled players aired their dirty laundry in public and clashed in private. It is the story of how the club's boardroom became the backdrop for an executive power struggle. And it is the story of how the magic touch that saw Carrick land the job permanently just five months ago deserted him...

Michael Carrick is out as Manchester United manager - just ten league games after guiding United to third and being handed a two-year contract

Carrick has had to turn up as normal at Carrington this week even as the world knew he was for the chop. Some dressing-room sources say he has been 'hung out to dry'

Carrick would have seen the writing on the wall when he got up to give an impressive speech at a dinner in the Heritage Suite at Old Trafford on Friday night to celebrate the 40th anniversary of Sir Alex Ferguson’s appointment.

The significance of the occasion was not lost on Carrick. Ferguson was brought in to succeed Ron Atkinson on the same date in November 1986 with United one place above the relegation zone. Having boxed himself into an identical corner, Carrick too was on borrowed time.

Two days later, he stood helplessly on the touchline at Old Trafford as United played out a ridiculous 4-4 draw with Aston Villa. Twice his team fought back from two goals down, but it was the final proof for United bosses that change was unavoidable.

The biggest club in English football having to endure the humiliation of at least two weeks in the relegation zone was too much for them to stomach.

His body language after the Bournemouth game when a tearful and crestfallen Carrick offered what looked like a farewell wave to the Stretford End – four days after he approached fans in Como to apologise for an equally painful 4-1 Champions League surrender – suggested he was already done.

The 45-year-old limped on to another defeat at Chelsea, then presided over a barmy 3-2 comeback win over Roma and the bonkers draw with Villa. A consummate and composed midfielder for United, Carrick of all people knew this wasn’t the way football should be played.

All the while, he spoke like a man who could turn things around. They always do. But the performances and results told a different story.

His critics would argue that someone whose only previous job in management was at Middlesbrough should never have been put in charge of United in the first place.

Others would say the bravest move by the club would have been to thank Carrick for his admirable efforts in securing third place and Champions League qualification last season, and move on in the summer when Pochettino would have been available for free before signing a new contract as US coach with a £7million release clause.

Matheus Cunha's late winner sealed a remarkable comeback win over Roma last week - but it promised to be a false dawn for Carrick

The 45-year-old's body language after an earlier loss to Bournemouth had suggested he was done back then, with Carrick appearing to wave farewell to the Stretford End

Instead, Carrick was rewarded with a two-year deal in May and the wheels fell off in spectacular fashion. Rather like his friend and former team-mate Ole Gunnar Solskjaer, the honeymoon period gave way to something altogether less appealing.

At least Solskjaer stuck around for three more years. Having beaten the Norwegian to the job in January, Carrick’s reign lasted less than 10 months.

The similarities between the two men are striking. Ex-players who basked in the glory days under Ferguson and know what it takes to succeed at Old Trafford, but were incapable of lifting the modern-day Manchester United to similar heights.

Having replaced Ruben Amorim and inspired a remarkable turnaround in the second half of last season, Carrick must have thought he had a chance.

His calm demeanour and experience of life in the Old Trafford pressure cooker that had played a part in his appointment was in contrast to the emotional incontinence of Amorim. How strange then that it has given way to such calamity and chaos.

United got off to the worst possible start by losing at newly-promoted Hull City in August. Tigers boss Sergej Jakirovic belittled them by saying his goalkeeper Konstantinos Tzolakis might as well have been sipping a cup of tea, and the hits have just kept on coming for Carrick.

Aside from Bournemouth, there were the late equalisers scored by Ainsley Maitland-Niles and Dominic Solanke for Everton and Tottenham, the controversial winner conceded to 10-man Manchester City, crashing out of the Carabao Cup to Brighton after giving up a two-goal lead, and the defeats in Europe by Atletico Madrid and Como after going behind in the opening seconds.

By the time Alysson scored in the first minute for Villa on Sunday, United’s defence had become as leaky as the Old Trafford roof. The four goals Carrick’s side conceded took it to 15 in his final four games.

United's defence has been leaky all season long, with Ainsley Maitland-Niles' stoppage-time equaliser at Hill Dickinson Stadium among the goals to cost them valuable points

By the time Alysson scored in the first minute for Aston Villa on Sunday, United’s defence had become as leaky as the Old Trafford roof. They let in 15 in his final four games

So why the alarming decline from last season? The United players haven’t looked sharp after a disjointed World Cup summer that involved a more relaxed one-week training camp in Ireland. There have been too many injuries.

Some players have spoken of a drop-off in training under Carrick and his backroom staff. Others feel that the switch to a more possession-based 4-2-4 system was asking too much of a group who aren’t suited to it.

There was surprise that a man so invested in the club's academy used so few young players. Harry Amass was left kicking his heels despite the chronic shortage of left backs, JJ Gabriel's shock exit was an unwelcome distraction, and Shea Lacey's re-emergence came too late.

It would be grossly unfair to pin this all on Carrick though. He is just the latest unfortunate to pay the price for the shambles at United which runs from the boardroom to the dressing room.

Amid ongoing uncertainty over his future, there has been a power struggle behind the scenes between director of football Jason Wilcox and director of recruitment Christopher Vivell over who to replace him, while sources close to the Glazer family have revealed a growing dissatisfaction at how minority owner Sir Jim Ratcliffe and Ineos are running the club.

United failed Carrick in the transfer market last summer with a futile pursuit of his No 1 midfield target Elliot Anderson, and instead delivered Youri Tielemans and Andrey Santos.

Carrick could see trouble on the horizon when he spoke to the media at Carton House in Maynooth during that pre-season camp in early August, but tried not to rock the boat.

It was the day his daughter Louise was getting her A-level results. ‘You know, we would like more, we need to keep pushing for more,’ he said, using the word ‘push’ another nine times.

There has been a power struggle behind the scenes between director of football Jason Wilcox (centre) and director of recruitment Christopher Vivell (left) over who to replace Carrick

Some players have spoken of a drop-off in training under Carrick and his backroom staff

Carlos Baleba followed but an ankle injury meant that he couldn’t play until October, and the £70m signing then suffered a hamstring injury.

United blundered catastrophically by focusing on midfield and delaying improvements to other areas of the team until next summer. In particular, the decision not to sign cover for Luke Shaw and Benjamin Sesko backfired spectacularly as both players succumbed to injury.

Lisandro Martinez called out United’s underwhelming recruitment in Madrid by saying he expected more of Wilcox, and he isn’t the only one who feels that way.

Tielemans gave a terse ‘no comment’ when he was asked on camera about his team-mate’s remarks, but it’s understood the two players clashed on the team flight back to Manchester as cracks began to show in an increasingly unhappy camp.

The camaraderie fostered by rounds of golf involving Carrick and the players during the break in Ireland was beginning to fall apart.

It came to a head when captain Bruno Fernandes became the second member of the leadership group to speak out of turn, questioning Carrick’s tactics after the loss to Como.

Having continuously defended his players in the face of criticism from some of his former United team-mates over their lack of effort, Carrick’s spiky response that ‘if you don't run forward and you don't run back, you aren’t going to win’ perhaps revealed his true feelings about this group.

He and Fernandes settled their differences in a clear-the-air meeting, but the questions didn’t go away. When he was asked about their relationship again at the end of what proved to be his final pre-match press conference last Friday, Carrick tapped his inquisitor on the shoulder as he left the room at Carrington and said with a smile: ‘Nice try!’

It was typical of Carrick, who often met questions with the knowing grin of a man who couldn’t say what he really thought.

Lisandro Martinez and Youri Tielemans clashed on United's flight back to Manchester following the former's incendiary comments after a loss to Atletico Madrid

One issue has been Carrick’s rather passionless appearance on the touchline. ‘He doesn't upset anyone, but doesn't inspire anyone either,’ remarked one dressing room source

It got to the point where he was encouraged by the club to be a little more forthcoming in press conferences. There have been some barbed post-match exchanges in TV interviews with Sky Sports and Viaplay this season, and the five-minute monologue before the Bournemouth game was pure theatre.

But on the whole, Carrick’s ability to play a straight bat in front of the cameras was a welcome change for the Old Trafford hierarchy compared to the loose-lipped Amorim – particularly with the Amazon Prime documentary-makers following their every move.

‘He talks a lot but doesn't say much,’ said Roy Keane, never Carrick’s biggest fan, on Sunday. ‘I don't know if that's a tactic, like a politician.’

More of an issue was Carrick’s rather passionless appearance on the touchline, which seemed to be reflected in his team at times.

Always dressed in black, a hand placed studiously on his chin, it has contrasted with more animated adversaries such as Unai Emery, Roberto De Zerbi and Diego Simeone in recent weeks.

‘He doesn't upset anyone, but doesn't inspire anyone either,’ remarked one dressing room source.

The fans were always behind him though, even the ones who may have accepted some time ago that a change was needed. Support and sympathy were never in short supply for a coach who has been let down by the men in the directors’ box and the players on the pitch.

That much has been abundantly clear by the abuse meted out to Ratcliffe and Co this season, and the unprecedented scenes when the fans turned on the players after Bournemouth.

Carrick looks set to be replaced by former Tottenham and Chelsea boss Mauricio Pochettino (right)

Carrick has been known to stand alongside them in the past, and it would be no surprise to see him there again in future.

‘Oh, it’s Carrick you know…’ echoed his tribute song from the Stretford End as he disappeared down the tunnel for the last time on Sunday evening.

So another bites the dust. A good man. A United man. The right man for the job? Maybe not, as it turns out. That poisoned chalice now passes to Mauricio Pochettino.

Six goals in 11 consecutive wins: Ex-Norwich City man stars for European club after £14m sale

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Norwich City bid farewell to Borja Sainz in the summer of 2025, following a fruitful two-year stay with the Norfolk outfit. NCFC signed the Spaniard from Turkish club Giresunspor and he enjoyed a steady first season with eight goals and two assists in 39 outings.

However, it was the 2024/25 campaign when Sainz would emerge as a real Championship star. The winger scored 19 goals and laid on four assists in 43 appearances for the Carrow Road club that season, prompting significant transfer interest before Porto paid a little over £14m to bring him in.

Sainz is now in his second season with the Portuguese giants. And, just like he did at Norwich City, the 25-year-old has grown into a starring role in his sophomore year.

Sainz had a solid debut year at Porto after joining from Norwich City, managing seven goals and six assists in 47 games across all competitions. He was not a nailed-on starter in Liga Portugal with 12 of his 30 league outings coming from the bench, but he settled well.

He has had to wait for his chances this term too, with all of his first eight league appearances coming off the bench despite scoring in three of those outings. Sainz has started the last three in the Portuguese top-flight though, and he has rewarded Francesco Farioli’s faith with three goals and an assist.

The ex-NCFC man has scored in back-to-back-to-back Liga Portugal games against Gil Vicente, Vitoria Guimaraes and Estoril, providing an assist against the latter too. He now has six goals and an assist to his name in 11 league matches this term.

Porto have won all 11 of those games to move into a commanding position at the top of the table. Sainz has made four appearances in the UEFA Champions League as well, but is yet to get off the mark in that prestigious competition.

Norwich City have had some real hits in the transfer market in recent times. Like Sainz, Gabriel Sara starred at Carrow Road and ended up earning a big move to Europe, while Mo Toure and Pelle Mattsson have become highly influential players at Carrow Road.

However, in terms of the money made and the level of success he found, Sainz takes some beating. He joined for nothing from Giresunspor and was sold for over £14m, making for a sizeable profit.

He’s gone on to prove his abilities after NCFC too, emerging as a key scorer at Porto. Sainz will be eager to keep delivering as Farioli and co see how long they can retain their perfect start to the Liga Portugal campaign.

Heaven’s Gate Cult Thriller ‘The Leader,’ Starring Vera Farmiga, Tim Blake Nelson and Jim Parsons, Bought for U.S. by Dark Sky Films (EXCLUSIVE)

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

On the 30th anniversary of the Heaven’s Gate tragedy — the infamous mass suicide involving members of a Californian religious cult — a film detailing the story is hitting cinemas.

“The Leader,” a true crime cult thriller starring Vera Farmiga, Tim Blake Nelson and Jim Parsons, has been picked for U.S. distribution by by Dark Sky Films, which plans to release the film in late March 2027, times to the 30th anniversary. Related Stories 'You've Got Mail' Reboot in the Works at Warner Bros. Pamela Abdy Delivers Fiery Speech After Warner Bros. Exit: ‘It Is Getting Harder, Not Easier, to Be a Woman in the Business’

From writer-director Michael Gallagher and also starring Grace Caroline Currey, and Simon Rex, the film had its world premiere at this year’s Tribeca Film Festival, landing critical praise. Variety’s review said it was the “most potent drama about life inside a cult since Martha Marcy May Marlene.”

The Exchange/Voltage is handling the remaining territories at AFM, with multiple major territories already sold to theatrical distributors.

As per the description, “The Leader” is set against the backdrop of “one of the most bizarre and devastating chapters in American history.” The story follows Marshall Herff Applewhite (Nelson), a former music teacher, and Bonnie Lu Nettles (Farmiga), a nurse whose chance meeting in the early 1970s sparks an extraordinary spiritual partnership. Convinced they have been chosen to guide humanity toward a higher plane of existence, the pair establish Heaven’s Gate, attracting followers willing to surrender their possessions, abandon their families, and sever ties with the outside world. “As their influence grows, devotion gives way to increasingly extreme beliefs, setting the group on a path toward an unimaginable conclusion,” adds the synopsis. “‘The Leader’ is the urgent question of why — and, in answer, the film becomes both a compelling psychological study and a chilling psychological horror.”

The distribution agreement was negotiated by Greg Newman, executive vice president of Dark Sky Films, and Brian O’Shea of The Exchange/Voltage Film Sales.

“True crime and cult stories continue to fascinate audiences, and Michael has delivered an exceptional film that is as compelling as it is unsettling, with a stellar cast delivering extraordinary performances,” said Newman. “For more than 20 years, we’ve built Dark Sky Films on a commitment to bold, distinctive storytelling, and ‘The Leader’ is a remarkable addition to that legacy. We couldn’t be prouder to bring this film to audiences.”

Added Justin DiPietro, executive vice president of MPI Media Group: “‘The Leader’ is an exceptional film whose critical acclaim speaks to both the strength of its storytelling and its broad audience appeal.”

Gallagher spent more than a decade researching Heaven’s Gate, drawing on historical records and archival footage to explore the psychology of cult influence, faith, and the human need for belonging.

“Heaven’s Gate has been an obsession of mine for nearly thirty years. When I was eight-years-old, 39 people died in my neighborhood. This event had a profound impact on my life. I wanted to make a film that used humanity to tell a real-life horror story— one that grabbed you and never let go,” he said. “I’m thrilled to partner with Dark Sky as we bring ‘The Leader’ to theaters nationwide.”

Said Hamza Ali, President of MPI Media Group: “The Leader’ is a powerful example of the kind of bold, thought-provoking filmmaking we want to bring to audiences. Michael has taken one of the most notorious stories in American history and created a gripping film that goes beyond the headlines to explore the people and psychology behind it. We’re excited to bring this extraordinary story to the big screen.”

“The Leader” is written and directed by Gallagher and produced by Jana Gallagher, Michael Wormser, Michael J. Gallagher, Joel David Moore, and Matt Murphie. Executive producers include Eric Gozlan and Ben Gozlan from Goldrush Entertainment, Norman Light, Evan Silverberg, James Cullen Bressack, Kelly Lynn Reiter, Stacy Kemp, Danielle Vasinova, Brian O’Shea, Sarah Dunn, Alexandra Cocean. The film features cinematography by Ben Mullen, production design by Tye Whipple, costume design by Zachary Sheets, editing by Joel Jay Blacker, casting by Lisa Essary CSA, and an original score by acclaimed horror composer Joseph Bishara. “The Leader”is produced by Cinemand Films, Balcony 9 Productions, Gold Rush Entertainment, Magnetic Labs, LB Entertainment, Red Barn Films, Copper Island, Silent Echo Productions, Drama Free Films and Entertainment 360.

May El-Toukhy’s Venice Golden Lion Winner ‘Woman Unknown’ Gets U.S. Deal During AFM

EXCLUSIVE: In a deal closing up at the American Film Market, Cohen Media Group has acquired U.S. distribution rights to Woman Unknown, the acclaimed feature from Danish filmmaker May el-Toukhy (Queen of Hearts), which won the Golden Lion for Best Film at Venice Film Festival, as well as the Volpi Cup for Best Actress for Mathilde Arcel.

The period drama, which has also been selected as Denmark’s official entry for Best International Feature Film, is set against the turbulent aftermath of World War II, and explores themes of ambition, identity, survival and the consequences of buried secrets. Related Stories News VMI Launches AFM Sales On Manila-Set Action Pic 'Big Tiger' With Luke Goss & Katherine McNamara News Leah McKendrick To Follow 'Voicemails For Isabelle' With "Ferociously Female" Thriller 'Delulu' Starring Maddie Ziegler; XYZ & Temple Hill Produce -- AFM

The acquisition was negotiated between Robert Aaronson, Executive Vice President of Cohen Media Group, and Susan Wendt, Managing Director of TrustNordisk, which is handling international sales.The official synopsis reads: “Marie, a young nanny and housemaid, is about to marry the wealthy older widower, Christian, for whom she is working. But Marie guards a shameful secret. During World War II, she was in an intimate relationship with a German soldier. Now Marie’s past threatens to catch up with her, and she is forced to do everything in her power to preserve her new status as lady of the house in post-war anarchy.” Watch on Deadline

The film has also played at the Toronto International Film Festival, BFI London Film Festival, Riga, Busan, Valladolid and Marrakech, among others. It is also on the fiction shortlist for the European Film Awards.

The film is directed by May el-Toukhy and written by el-Toukhy and Maren Louise Käehne. It is produced by Mikael Rieks (Land of Mine) for Nordisk Film Production A/S, in co-production with Nafta Films, Nordisk Film Production Sweden and Film i Väst.

Nordisk Film Distribution is handling local distribution, while TrustNordisk represents international sales.

Robert Aaronson, EVP of Cohen Media Group, stated: “May el-Toukhy has created an extraordinary film that combines the sweep of a historical drama with the intimacy and emotional complexity of a deeply personal story. Woman Unknown is a powerful exploration of survival, identity and the choices people make when confronted by their past, anchored by a remarkable performance from Mathilde Arcel. Its success at Venice and the recognition it has received internationally speak to the film’s exceptional artistry. We are thrilled to partner with TrustNordisk to bring this compelling work to American audiences.”

Susan Wendt, Managing Director of TrustNordisk, added: “We are really looking forward to our cooperation. Cohen’s enthusiasm for the film is very motivating and means a lot, and I am confident that they will do an excellent job of bringing the film to the market and to the American audiences.”

The filmmaker’s 2019 drama Queen of Hearts (2019) premiered at the Sundance Film Festival and received numerous international honors, including the Audience Award at Sundance, the Nordic Council Film Prize and three awards at the Göteborg Film Festival. El-Toukhy has also directed episodes of TV series including The Legacy, Ride Upon the Storm, Cry Wolf and Netflix’s The Crown.

Previously sold territories for Unknown Woman include UK and Ireland (Vue Lumière) Germany and Austria (Weltkino), France (Memento), Spain (Karma Films), Poland (Best Film), Portugal (Nitrato Filmes), the former Yugoslavia (MCF Megacom Film), Switzerland (Filmcoopi), Bulgaria (Beta Film), Italy (Lucky Red), Benelux (September Film), Latin America (California Filmes), Greece and Cyprus (Neo Films) and the Baltics (Estin Film).

The production received support from the Danish Film Institute, Eurimages – Council of Europe, the Investment and Development Agency of Latvia, Riga City Council, Nordisk Film & TV Fund, DR, the Swedish Film Institute, SVT, LIAA, Riga Film Fund, TrustNordisk and Zentropa.
