Jeremy O. Harris EPs ‘American Theater,’ About a Canceled MAGA Theater Director’s Bizarre Attempted Comeback — Watch the Trailer

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Can a conservative “canceled” theater director rise from the ashes of his own ruination — and in this climate? That’s one of the questions top of mind for filmmakers Nicholas Clark and Dylan Frederick, whose provocative, Christopher Guest-style nonfiction film “American Theater” (except it’s a docu-, not mockumentary) is rolling out in select theaters now. A Slamdance Grand Jury Prize winner for documentary in 2025, the film is also executive-produced by Jeremy O. Harris.

“American Theater” follows Brian Clowdus — openly gay, conservative, and formerly of the Serenbe Playhouse in Georgia, who was taken down in 2019 over allegations of racism, misconduct, unsafe working conditions, and a gamut of other accusations. Clark and Frederick follow Clowdus, now a MAGA Republican, and a troupe of other conservative actors to an abandoned cabin in middle-of-nowhere Georgia where Clowdus plots the ultimate revenge: a community musical theater retelling of, of course, the 1692 Salem witch trials, riffing on Arthur Miller’s “The Crucible” (which, published in 1953, isn’t yet in the public domain). Related Stories Fan Edits Are Legal with Moonbeem, Distribution Network Hosts Approved Clips from Filmmakers ‘How to Rob a Bank’ Review: David Leitch’s Fun and Poppy YouTube Heist Movie Almost Gets Away with It

“This film was about a really fucked-up figure and a person with really fucked-up politics,” Harris told IndieWire over the phone from Copenhagen, where the Tony nominee is rehearsing a new play. “When you have unwelcoming and fucked-up politics, or a person who has unwelcoming, fucked-up politics at the center of the art you’re making, it can feel like potentially you’re endorsing their politics or celebrating them, especially because we don’t live in times that are deeply discursive or critical. At least having my name in the headline should be an invitation to certain people that maybe would’ve been like, ‘I’m not watching that,’ that maybe this is something for them.”

In taking on someone who was once a formidable presence in the Southern regional theater space, Harris said the filmmakers saw “this opportunity to embed themselves in the company for his comeback show. They realized there was a chance to look at the type of fringe character that utilized MAGA for the grifting machine that it is. MAGA is a magnet for a lot of displaced people, and theater has so many displaced people. Even thinking about the fact that one of the most famous people that stormed the Capitol was in the regional production of ‘Jesus Christ Superstar’ meant that there were ways in which the theater community wasn’t looking at some of the foxes among the lambs in our own community. This gave them the opportunity to be those foxes, or not even foxes, but to be flies on the wall of this maker who was exploiting a lot of displaced people.”

A recent Cobble Hill Cinemas screening of “American Theater” was moderated by journalist Ludwig Hurtado. Next up, the film will be available to rent on the Letterboxd Video Store, so locally driven screenings also aim to bring audiences to that platform.

“One of the things that’s very sad to me is that amongst the distributors, the ones that one would think have the most bandwidth and power to really uplift these microbudget filmmakers who are truly keeping the torch burning for that independent American brio that put American independent cinema on the map and right next to so many other stalwarts of world cinema, a lot of them are being, I think, more cautious about who they’re distributing,” Harris said. “Distribution costs are so large right now that I think that they don’t really know what to do with movies so small. And so Letterboxd has become this really great outlet because there’s such a vibrant community there who are just excited to see films and excited to learn that filmmakers who maybe are in circumstances similar to their own as movie watchers are making films.”

In a separate statement, Frederick said, “The film takes place in 2021, when Trump was facing the possibility of spending the rest of his life in prison, his political future was uncertain, and conservatives were batching DeSantis ‘24 tank tops. Democrats just had the best night of their lives in the midterms, and MAGA folks may find themselves at a low point, but it’s useful to remember that we’ve actually been here before.”

Clark added, “There was a moment when many people assumed the Trump era had come to an end. ‘American Theater’ tells a story of the Trump supporters during that time who were dedicated to figuring out what would come next. In this instance, their idea was an allegorical cancel culture musical about the Salem witch trials. It did not go anywhere near as planned, and our movie tells that story. But ultimately, everything that has happened to us as a nation ever since has only recontextualized what we saw within this brief moment in time.”

Watch the trailer for “American Theater” below, and see a list of upcoming screenings on a nationwide tour via Antenna Releasing.

Trisha Paytas is just raking in the money off her kitchen floor.

New Richard Avedon Documentary Shows That Fashion Isn’t All Style—It’s Substance, Too

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

When The Devil Wears Prada 2 hit theaters this past spring, it painted an accurate and depressing picture of how fashion publishing has dwindled, its creative juices squeezed. Miranda Priestly, is no longer calling on her assistants to ring Patrick, a reference to the fashion photographer Patrick Demarchelier, or anyone of that caliber.

As someone who spent a majority of their career in that industry and had a short stint at Vogue, the publication the “Devil Wears Prada” films were based on, it’s fair to say everyone goes in with a dream of what it means to work at a fashion magazine. Often, those fantasies are informed by the photographs of Herb Ritts, Helmut Newton, and Richard Avedon, the latter of which is the subject of a new documentary by filmmaker Ron Howard. Titled Avedon, it releases on Netflix today. Related Articles Netflix Acquires Richard Avedon Documentary for 2026 Release After Cannes Debut Netflix Is Developing a Series About Frida Kahlo and Diego Rivera

As far as fashion documentaries go, it’s full of style and substance, backed up with anecdotes from Larry Gagosian, Tina Brown, Twiggy, Hilton Als, Samira Nasr, Isabella Rossellini, Calvin Klein, Avedon’s son John, and James Martin, the executive director of the Richard Avedon Foundation. Gagosian, who represents the Richard Avedon Foundation and has just opened an Avedon show in Beverly Hills, recalls going to brunch with the photographer, where all that was served was hard-boiled eggs and champagne. “I thought it was super elegant,” says Gagosian.

Using a combination of archival footage and interviews, Howard echoes the glitz and extravagance of fashion publishing’s bygone era of hefty budgets and creative freedom: Avedon’s photoshoots for Harper’s Bazaar and Vogue would last four days, and often featured models such as Lauren Hutton, Beverly Johnson, and Penelope Tree. Avedon managed to capture not just pretty faces, but the souls and secrets behind them.

Avedon grew up reading magazines such as Harper’s Bazaar, Vanity Fair, and Vogue at his father’s women’s retail business on Fifth Avenue. In them, he found the photographs by Martin Munkácsi, Edward Steichen, and Anton Bruehl.

He got his start in the 1940s, during World War II, working as a staff photographer for the United States Maritime Service, for which photographed around 100,000 faces on plain white backgrounds for ID cards with his Rolleiflex camera. “It’s existential white, the only thing you see is the geography of the face,” Avedon says in an old interview used in the film, referring to the way he spotlit his subjects against a white background.

After the war, he took courses in graphic design and photography at the New School, where he met Harper’s Bazaar’s art director, Alexey Brodovitch, who encouraged him to go to Paris in 1947 as part of the magazine’s mandate of “bringing back the glamor of pre-war Paris.”

At that publication, Avedon bore witness to a fashion revolution. In 1947, Christian Dior presented his New Look collection, an antidote to the rationed fabrics of the war, with full pleated skirts falling below mid-calf length. Avedon, who extravagantly captured five girls swirling in their full-length skirts, says that he lit each photograph he took in Paris as though he were reconstructing a Fred Astaire and Ginger Rogers film.

He recalls photographing a model crying at a railway station. The picture was met with some acerbic feedback from Carmel Snow, the renowned editor-in-chief of Harper’s Bazaar, who coined the term the New Look: “Nobody cries in a Dior hat, Dick.”

Avedon’s work for Harper’s Bazaar shaped his career, paving the way for him to become a star beyond the fashion world. In 1955, he photographed one of his most famous images, Dovima with elephants; Gagosian reveals he once sold it for $2 million. By 1957, Avedon had shot to fame like Picasso (he photographed the artist in 1958) and had become the inspiration behind Stanley Donen’s film Funny Face, in which an Avedon-like photographer is played by Astaire. In a nod to the film’s inspiration, Avedon was brought on to shoot the stills used for the film’s opening credits.

Fundamentally, Avedon was always on a mission to capture the human condition. He firstly did so with stars and starlets to illustrate that nothing is really so perfect. Howard’s documentary seems to suggest that this was all related to Avedon’s own imperfect upbringing.

In Howard’s documentary, Avedon, who died in 2004, says that his parents always presented an idealized family in their scrapbooks and often borrowed a dog as a prop to exaggerate the image they cultivated. He grew up in a stern household with a father that was emotionally absent for a big chunk of his life until they reconciled later in life.

His sister Louise, was his first muse. Avedon says in the film that her “beauty affected me,” and that all the models he chose resembled his sister with their oval faces, high cheekbones, and brunette hair. Louise was catatonic and spent more than 15 years living in a psychiatric hospital, where she died in 1968 at age 42.

“One of the great themes in his work is alienation and isolation. He wants to know what’s going on inside human beings,” says John, Avedon’s son. In times of heartache, Avedon reveled in his work—such as when his first wife, the actress Doe Avedon, left him for another man that was blonde and tall, or when his second wife, Evelyn Franklin, fell into depression, leading Avedon to start living out of his studio. “Everything diminishes,” Avedon says here. “Work does not. It’s the one thing that doesn’t turn on you.”

Maybe because his life wasn’t so pretty, Avedon’s photographs didn’t always flatter. When he shot Nancy Reagan, for example, he abided by all of her handler’s guidelines. Then, in the final image, he focused on her claw-like hands, a contrast to her perfectly, smooth face.

Sometimes to get the image he wanted, he would manipulate his subjects, as he did when he shot the Duke and Duchess of Windsor (formerly King Edward VIII and Wallis Simpson). Avedon told them that, on the way to the photoshoot, his taxi ran over a dog. This was a lie, but they seemed to believe it, and Avedon captured the droop in their smile. Simpson told Avedon that she would destroy him when the photos were published, but Avedon, who was Jewish, described the couple as “compassionate,” despite the fact that they sympathized with the Nazis during World War II.

It’s evident through Avedon’s line of work that the photographer saw the world beyond the pages of Harper’s Bazaar and Vogue. He waited seven weeks in Vietnam to photograph the American generals of the war; the residents of the American West and the Chicago 7 also counted among his subjects. The film portrays Avedon as a complicated figure, one whose contributions exceed the field of fashion photography.

It also portrays him as a complicated person, but it could’ve gone even further. Howard shies away from claims about Avedon’s sexuality that have been voiced in books such as Avedon: Something Personal, by Norma Stevens and Steven M. L. Aronson, and Michael Gross’s Focus: The Secret, Sexy, Sometimes Sordid World of Fashion Photographers. Gross reports that Avedon had an affair with his high school classmate, the writer James Baldwin, who even worked on a book with Avedon called Nothing Personal. That isn’t addressed here. (Avedon’s foundation vigorously disputed certain episodes described in Something Personal upon the book’s release, but it did not outright deny the descriptions of the photographer’s sexual identity.)

Howard also glosses over the fact that Avedon stopped working for Vogue when Anna Wintour replaced Grace Mirabella as editor-in-chief, moving away from his cropped headshot covers. This is also skipped over entirely.

Howard is instead more focused on the glamorous ghosts of a vanished era. The fashion industry has been consumed by capitalism, and fashion magazines have lost their ability to influence. All we’re left with is tales of the good old days and a graveyard of nostalgic images. You don’t have to look too closely to see that the devil is in the picture. For proof, just look at one of the photographs featured here: Avedon’s 1952 portrait of the actor Charlie Chaplin, who raises two fingers to his head, as though he were Satan himself.

Trump wanted little Kei trucks for America, Toyota makes it happen

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Does this herald the start of a small car revolution, or is it a meaningless distraction?

The idea that Americans should be offered a greater choice of smaller cars has support from strange bedfellows indeed. After all, who could forget President Trump—usually one to take a maximalist approach to any problem—announcing last year that he wanted the Department of Transportation to make Japan’s diminutive “Kei” cars legal here in the US.

Ars’ attempts to get DoT to tell us more about the potential influx of little vehicles have mostly fallen on deaf ears. But this week, there has been movement on the topic, with Toyota announcing that it will bring back the Scion brand with the KEI-01.

Here in the US, the preponderance of humongous SUVs is a consequence largely of government policies, specifically corporate average fuel economy standards that made it less profitable to sell smaller, efficient cars. Japan’s Kei cars are what happens if the government instead restricts the size, engine capacity, and power output.

A Kei car must fit within the following dimensions: 11.2 feet (3.4 m) long, 4.9 feet (1.5 m) wide, and 6.6 feet (2 m) tall, with an engine displacement of no more than 0.66 L and an output of 63 hp (47 kW) or less. That’s a far cry from the more than 16 feet (4.9 m) of a full-size pickup truck, but it makes sense for a country where you’re not allowed to buy a car unless you have somewhere to park it, with those spaces being at an absolute premium.

Being affordable to buy and economical to run sure doesn’t hurt either, and last year, Japan bought almost 1.7 million Kei cars, out of total sales of 4.6 million vehicles. Depending on where you live in the US, it can be varying degrees of difficult to import a Kei car, at least until now.

Toyota killed off its Scion sub-brand more than a decade ago, ending the haggle-free approach and cars aimed at younger buyers in favor of selling more Toyotas. But now it’s back as “Scion by Toyota,” which the automaker will apparently use to experiment with less traditional ideas. But forget about a cool-but-boxy EV in the mold of the Honda N-One or Nissan Sakura.

“The future of mobility will not be defined by one vehicle, one technology, or one way of thinking,” said Simon Humphries, Toyota’s chief branding officer and head of design. “Scion by Toyota gives us a place to challenge conventional thinking, explore new possibilities, and create mobility in new ways. The KEI-01, SxS, and BKE concepts each represent a different possibility—and together, they show what can happen when curiosity leads the way.”

Before anyone gets too excited, the KEI-01 will be classified as a “low-speed vehicle,” which means it can’t go any faster than 25 mph (40 km/h). So if you were hoping to use one of these but need to drive on anything but surface streets, be prepared to get honked at, a lot, and not in a good way. In fact, unless you’re buying a fleet, Toyota won’t sell you a KEI-01 at all, at least not to begin with.

Oh, and it’s only available as right-hand drive, since that’s the side of the road Japan drives on. But it does feature all-wheel drive, so if you own a ranch—or perhaps a whole load of golf courses—the utility becomes a little more obvious. Toyota says the KEI-01 is suited to small business owners in urban environments, farmers and ranchers, organizations that have campuses that need fleets of facility vehicles, delivery drivers, and outdoor enthusiasts. The trucks will be shipped to Toyota’s assembly plant in Indiana as knock-down kits, adding 50 jobs to the plant in mid-2027.

Low-speed vehicles have been street-legal in parts of the US for many years now. In my neighborhood in Washington, DC, Circuit operates a low-speed EV shuttle on demand, and gas-powered golf carts are very popular in retirement communities. Pricing for the KEI-01 hasn’t been announced, but speculation runs from as little as $10,000 to as much as $28,000.

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

CFTC is warning Kalshi it could face charges over contract filing failures

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Kalshi calls the issue a procedural error involving a handful of contracts, many of which were not live, and says it is addressing it

Kalshi has received a warning from the Commodity Futures Trading Commission that it could face charges over how it filed new event contracts with the agency, according to Reuters, which cited Politico. The CFTC’s notification suggests charges may be imminent.

The CFTC is examining whether Kalshi neglected to notify it about event contracts the company planned to list, Kalshi confirmed to CNBC. The company said the agency singled out contracts tied to weather, and that it is cooperating with the regulator to resolve the issue. Kalshi did not say when the investigation began.

"The notice is about a procedural error involving the submission of a handful of contracts, many of which weren't live," Elisabeth Diana, head of communications at Kalshi, said, according to CNBC. Kalshi declined to comment to CNBC on possible charges.

Reuters reported that the probe covers dozens of new products, while Kalshi described it as involving a handful of contracts.

The CFTC had not responded to requests for comment from Reuters or CNBC when they published.

Prediction market platforms may launch event contracts on their own certification, with no need for advance CFTC sign-off, provided they follow the Commodity Exchange Act, according to CNBC. That process allows new contracts to be introduced faster.

Back in August, the CFTC directed Kalshi to remove sports-related mention markets, which let traders wager on the words individuals use during a broadcast or speech, while the agency studied that contract type, according to CNBC.

Never Paid Into Social Security? Here's How You Might Qualify for Benefits Regardless

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Social Security benefits typically have to be earned, but there's another option if you don't have a work history.

Social Security is a lifeline for millions of older Americans today. And for many people, the path to being eligible for benefits involves working and paying into the system.

Social Security's primary source of revenue is payroll taxes. As such, to qualify for benefits in retirement, you need to pay taxes on a certain amount of wages to accumulate work credits.

In 2026, for example, the value of a single Social Security work credit is $1,890. In 2027, that value is rising to $1,970.

You can collect a maximum of four Social Security work credits per calendar year, and you need 40 total work credits in your lifetime to receive benefits in retirement. But if you never worked or have a minimal earnings history, you may not have those 40 credits by the time your senior years roll around.

The good news is that there may be another path toward collecting Social Security. It's important to know what options you have if your earnings history does not render you eligible for benefits.

If you're married to someone who's eligible for Social Security benefits or are an eligible divorcee, you may be entitled to collect spousal Social Security benefits. But it's important to know how they work.

First, if you're married, you can't file for spousal Social Security benefits until your spouse signs up. If you're divorced, you may be able to get your spousal benefits before your former spouse files. Your ex-spouse also can't prevent you from claiming spousal benefits, and your claim doesn't impact the amount of money they get.

Secondly, spousal Social Security benefits max out at 50% of your current or former spouse's full retirement age benefit. And you can claim your spousal benefits in full at your full retirement age.

In other words, if your spouse gets $2,000 at their full retirement age, your spousal benefit is worth $1,000 if you claim it at your full retirement age. If you file early, which you can do starting at age 62, your spousal benefit will be reduced for life.

You also can't boost your spousal benefit by delaying your claim past full retirement age. Delayed retirement credits are available to Social Security recipients claiming benefits based on their own earnings records, but they don't apply to spousal benefits.

If you never worked or barely worked, you might assume that Social Security is off the table. But you may be entitled to spousal Social Security benefits that can serve as a key source of income later in life.

The key is to make sure you understand how those benefits work, what they're worth, and when to file. The more you know about spousal benefits, the more helpful they might be to your finances.

Over the last month, Avalanche's price has declined by more than 21%, bottoming out at about $7.82.

From Local to Global: Anshuman Jha & Sarah-Jane Dias On Advocating Animal Rights In ‘Lakadbaggha 2’ & Uniqueness Of Crafting An Indian Action Franchise Rooted In Local Marital Arts

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Deadline’s Local to Global series touched down in Bruges, Belgium, last week where we sat down with actor-director Anshuman Jha and actress Sarah-Jane Dias to discuss their upcoming Indian action title Lakadbaggha 2: The Monkey Business. The film, which had its world premiere at the Razor Reel Flanders Film Festival where it was the runner-up for the Audience Choice Award, is the second instalment of Jha’s franchise and intersects local Indian mythology with indigenous martial arts all while advocating for animal conservation.

The project is produced by First Ray Films and Matta Cinema and follows Arjun Bakshi (Jha) who, weeks before his wedding, is shattered when his fiancée (Dias) disappears while on a mission in North Sulawesi, Indonesia. Her trail leads to a deadly island where an underground fight tournament hides the auction of the last surviving Yaki monkey – forcing Arjun to fight not just for love, but for a life that cannot speak for itself. Related Stories News Deadline From Local To Global: Producers Of 'Gohan', '9 Temples To Heaven' & More Discuss How Thai Cinema Is Travelling News International Insider: Cannes Gets Cracking; Deadline Goes Local To Global; Barry Keoghan Interview

“I feel that animals are going extinct because humanity is going extinct,” said Jha. “So, the next evolution of heroes or ‘superheroes’ is going to be somebody who’s not more powerful, but somebody who will have a lot more compassion. Being ordinary is the real superpower and that was the root of the birth story of Arjun Bakshi.”

The first instalment of the Hindi-language franchise sees vegan Arjun travel to Kolkata where he turns into a vigilante after discovering an illegal underground animal trade syndicate. The latest film heads to Indonesia, where it puts a spotlight on the Yaki Monkey, whose population has been reduced from 50,000 in1990 to 4,500 today. “This is a real-world problem,” said Jha. “I feel films need to not just entertain but also have a voice, especially for those who are voiceless and this story is in essence about that.”

India has a rich martial arts heritage, particularly through Kalaripayattu, also known as Kalari, which Jha says has rarely been conveyed on screen.

“We are the second biggest film industry in the world, so technically there should be more films featuring this,” he says. “The effort was to bring a very 1980s pulp action genre fan to the screen. I’m a huge Bruce Lee fan. He’s had a huge influence on my life and a lot of who I am, what I believe in and how I live goes in alignment with the principles of martial arts.”

Dias noted the film’s unconventional approach to portraying an action hero: “Quintessentially, when there is fighting in a film, it’s over love or someone wanting to take over the world or they are some dark force, but I have never seen it done to protect animals or the environment, especially in a superhero action film and I think that’s actually really unique.”

Jha worked with master fight choreographer Kecha Khamphakdee, who is behind the iconic fight scenes in Ong-Bak, to design the combat scenes in the latest film. Tony Jaa, the star of Ong-Bak: Muay Thai Warrior makes his Bollywood/Hindi film debut in the film while Dan Chupong, another star from the Ong-Bak world, plays the antagonist in the film.

When pressed about the challenges of Indian films traveling outside its own borders, Jha stressed that he was conscious of this when making the film and hopes it’s a story that Western audiences can embrace because of its universal themes and the fact that it’s an atypical Indian independent film.

“I want this film to travel the world,” says Jha. “I feel like there’s an emotional quotient – EQ – and there’s cinema quotient. I think because we have 1.6 billion people in India, sometimes as filmmakers we get very happy with just what we have. The truth is, there are 5.4 billion people outside of India. So, there’s a huge audience out there and the cinema quotient of audiences outside of India is very different from the inside of India.”

Contemporary Beadworker Ken Williams Jr Awarded Rakow Commission by Corning Museum of Glass

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Marking the prize’s 40th year, "I Am the Hungry Cat" is the first Rakow Commission awarded for beadwork.

The Corning Museum of Glass selected Northern Arapaho and Cattaraugus Seneca artist Ken Williams Jr as the recipient of the 2026 Rakow Commission. Williams began learning beadwork at an early age on the Cattaraugus Seneca Indian Territory and later refined his technique alongside master beadworkers from his mother’s Northern Arapaho family.

Widely celebrated for his pictorial beadwork, Williams used the Commission as an opportunity to move beyond functional and wearable forms, creating a standalone wall panel. Utilizing 68 different colors from a range of historic to contemporary made beads, the framed composition features a graphic depiction of the artist’s cat, Valentino, complete with thought bubbles depicting his favorite foods as part of his “Yum Series.” In centering imagery of his cat and favorite foods detailed in bold colors and expressive lines, Williams is actually presenting a self-portrait through the work.

Now on view in the Museum’s Contemporary Art + Design Gallery, I Am the Hungry Cat, draws inspiration from Pop Art and a vibrant color palette and bridges ancestral artistic traditions with a playful contemporary sensibility, expanding the possibilities of glass as a medium for narrative art.

The Rakow Commission is one of the most prestigious annual awards for artists working in glass and supports the creation of a new work by encouraging artists to venture into technical or conceptual projects that they would have been unable to explore due to financial limitations. The annual program was endowed by the late Dr. and Mrs. Leonard S. Rakow, who were Fellows, benefactors, and dear friends of the Museum.

In its 40th year, the annual award was first presented in 1986, and recipients ranged from emerging to established artists including Te Rongo Kirkwood, Charisse Pearlina Weston, Leo Tecosky, Preston Singletary, Silvia Levenson, Klaus Moje, and Lino Tagliapietra.

Learn more at info.cmog.org/rakow-commission.

This residency at the U-M Stamps School, in partnership with the U-M Museum of Art (UMMA), offers artists a $20,000 honorarium, housing, studio space, and a culminating exhibition in UMMA’s prominent Stenn Gallery.

Is AIIB's complaint mechanism failing affected communities?

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The AIIB has a grievance redress mechanism for people affected by projects it finances. But those seeking to file a complaint face significant hurdles. Critics say reforms and greater transparency are needed.

For members of the Indigenous Sasak community on the Indonesian island of Lombok, the conflict over their land is not over.

For them, a tourism and infrastructure project co-financed by the Asian Infrastructure Investment Bank (AIIB) is a source of frustration, as they believe it has led to the expropriation of their land and destruction of their livelihoods.

In late September, they officially filed a complaint with the bank. It remains unclear whether this complaint will lead to an independent investigation.

The AIIB, which finances major infrastructure projects across Asia, has a mechanism in place to deal with such complaints.

Individuals who suffer adverse effects from the bank-financed projects may, under certain conditions, file a complaint with the institution.

However, access to this process — known as the Project Complaints Mechanism (PPM) — remains controversial.

According to a report by The Diplomat, a magazine that covers political and economic developments in Asia, none of the 45 complaints listed on the AIIB's website had been deemed admissible as of mid-September.

The bank acknowledged that "no cases have passed the PPM's eligibility assessment," but it stressed that it is "only part of the story."

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The AIIB emphasized that not every issue needs to be resolved through this mechanism.

The PPM is "only one part of AIIB's grievance redress ecosystem," the Beijing-based bank said in response to a query from DW.

Those affected should first attempt to resolve concerns directly with the project team at the local level, it said.

According to the bank, "twelve complaints were in fact resolved at the project level — as a direct result of AIIB's follow-up and intervention — and one was resolved through domestic courts."

But critics see this as a major weakness.

"The mechanism must be easily accessible," said Arntraud Hartmann, a development economist at the Johns Hopkins University School of Advanced International Studies (SAIS Europe) in Bologna, Italy.

She argued that people affected by the projects, for instance, are required to document potential violations of environmental and social standards from the start. "How is the affected party supposed to know which standards might have been violated?" she questioned.

The AIIB disputes this claim. "In terms of accessibility, we have made the submission form simpler and translated it into 12 languages," it said, adding that complaints can be submitted online, by phone, or by email.

Urgewald, a German environmental NGO, cites another problem with AIIB-financed projects.

For many of them, information on environmental impacts and resettlements is either missing or published late, it noted.

For 2024, the NGO found that 11 of the 16 projects it examined lacked complete environmental and social impact assessments. In many cases, information on the potential number of displaced residents was also missing, it added.

"The information published on the bank's website is often insufficient and inconsistent. Particularly important documents, such as environmental impact assessments or resettlement plans, vary widely or are missing entirely," said Nora Sausmikat, a China expert at Urgewald.

The lack of information makes it difficult for those affected to respond in a timely manner and report violations early on, she underscored, adding that the situation is particularly difficult for people without formal land titles.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Christoph Steinert, a political scientist at the University of Konstanz, said the mere existence of such grievance redressal mechanisms "is not enough."

Particularly vulnerable groups often know little about their rights, he said, adding that information should be available in local languages and targeted outreach directed at affected communities.

Criticism of the AIIB's procedures is not new.

A 2023 report by international aid organizations, including Recourse, Accountability Counsel, and Inclusive Development International, revealed that the AIIB's grievance mechanism had failed to accept a single complaint since its 2019 inception.

The study highlighted that 101 of the 219 AIIB-financed projects, or 46% of of the total projects at the time, were ineligible for review due to co-financing exemptions.

The AIIB typically provides funding without leading projects, a standard practice in development finance. When complaints arise, it directs complainants to the lead lender. As a result, nine of the 45 complaints fell outside its jurisdiction.

For Sausmikat, this is part of the problem. "The AIIB, however, decided against revising this requirement and established a mechanism that is very difficult to access," she said.

As of January 1, 2026, following two years of external consultations, the AIIB reformed its complaints mechanism. Under certain conditions, complaints can now be filed even while legal proceedings are ongoing.

But Sausmikat stressed that the changes are insufficient. Of the eight key recommendations made by an independent expert, only three were adopted, she underlined. "The revision did not remove the actual obstacles."

The AIIB stated that the reforms are intended to make the mechanism "more accessible and responsive."

The new rules have been in effect for only ten months, and their implementation is being closely monitored, the bank said.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Steinert said fear of reprisals poses another fundamental challenge to such procedures.

Documented cases from Latin America, for instance, show that complainants have faced administrative or legal problems, and in some cases even violence.

Such risks could deter others from filing complaints, Steinert said.

However, his research does not specifically address AIIB projects.

The bank said that it "does not tolerate retaliation against anyone who raises concerns."

"Requestors may ask for confidentiality, and the revised PPM policy strengthens protection against reprisals," it added.

Sausmikat said Germany — as a co-founder and the fourth-largest shareholder of the AIIB — could also play a role, working with other member states to push for changes.

Hartmann also stressed that the bank's shareholders bear responsibility and urged the AIIB's Board of Directors to amend the rules governing the grievance mechanism. "We need a policy based on good-practice standards."

This article was originally written in German.

Lane splitting laws: how 10 U.S. and global rules differ for riders

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Lane splitting laws vary widely, from California's broad acceptance to Utah's narrow filtering rules, and this guide explains how the practice works and where riders can use it

A motorcyclist riding between two lanes of slow freeway traffic in California is using a maneuver the state's vehicle code now defines by name. In most other U.S. states, the same maneuver is prohibited. That gap is why lane splitting laws draw questions from riders, drivers and anyone planning a road trip across state lines.

Part of the confusion is vocabulary. Lane splitting, lane filtering and lane sharing sound alike, but they describe different things. Splitting usually means riding between two lanes of traffic moving in the same direction. Filtering usually means moving between stopped or slow vehicles, often at a red light. Sharing means two motorcycles riding side by side in one lane. Some states and countries draw these lines differently. A maneuver allowed under one name in one place may carry a different label somewhere else.

The U.S. picture is uneven. California has long accepted lane splitting without an explicit ban. Assembly Bill 51, signed in 2016, added a formal definition of the practice to the California Vehicle Code. It also directed the California Highway Patrol to develop educational safety guidelines. Those guidelines are not binding law, and state law sets no fixed speed limit for the practice. Utah's narrower filtering law took effect in 2019. It allows the practice only in stopped traffic, on roads with lower posted speed limits and at low riding speeds. Arizona, Montana and Colorado have their own filtering laws, each with conditions. Most other states prohibit the practice, either by statute or through rules that keep vehicles within a single lane.

Outside the U.S., the approach shifts again. The U.K.'s Highway Code addresses filtering in slow-moving traffic. France introduced a legal filtering experiment in 2021 in certain departments. In Australia, rules vary by state, and New South Wales allows filtering under set conditions.

The 10 slides that follow start with the basic definitions. They then move through the U.S. states with specific rules, the reasons most states ban the practice and the approaches taken abroad. The final slide explains the mechanics that matter for safety: the speed difference between a motorcycle and surrounding traffic, driver awareness and mirror checks.

Lane splitting is a motorcyclist riding between two lanes of traffic that are moving in the same direction. The rider travels along the line that divides those lanes, passing cars and trucks on both sides instead of waiting in a single lane behind them.

The maneuver depends on width. A motorcycle takes up far less of the road than a car, a pickup or a delivery van. That difference leaves a strip of usable space between vehicles when they sit side by side in neighboring lanes. A rider can guide the bike through that strip.

In practice, the rider lines the motorcycle up with the painted divider and moves forward through the gap between two rows of vehicles. The pinch points are usually where side mirrors stick out from cars and trucks. Larger vehicles such as buses and tractor-trailers narrow the gap further. When the space runs out or traffic picks up, the rider merges back into a regular lane.

The definition has firm edges. Both lanes must carry traffic going the same way. Crossing a center line to pass into oncoming traffic is ordinary overtaking, not lane splitting. Riding on a paved shoulder or in a bike lane is a separate act as well.

The practice tends to show up where roads have several lanes and traffic slows down. Multi-lane freeways at rush hour and wide city streets with long lines at signals are typical settings. On a two-lane rural road with one lane in each direction, there is no pair of same-direction lanes to split.

Riders use lane splitting mainly to keep moving when traffic stalls. A motorcycle stuck in stop-and-go traffic gains nothing from its small size. Riders on air-cooled bikes also deal with engine heat building up at low speed. In hot climates, riders sit exposed to the sun and to exhaust from the vehicles around them.

Whether the maneuver is allowed depends on the state or country where the rider is on the road. Some places accept it broadly, while others prohibit it outright.

Lane splitting, lane filtering and lane sharing are three different motorcycle maneuvers. Splitting is riding between two lanes of same-direction traffic, filtering is moving between stopped or slow traffic, and sharing is two motorcycles side by side in one lane.

The difference between splitting and filtering comes down to what the surrounding traffic is doing. In lane splitting, the cars on either side are moving. The rider travels in the gap between them, along the line that separates the two lanes.

Lane filtering typically happens when that traffic has stopped or slowed to a crawl. The classic example is a line of cars waiting at a red light. A rider who filters works forward between the stationary vehicles, often toward the front of the queue.

Lane sharing is a separate idea. It does not involve passing anyone. Two motorcycles occupy a single lane at the same time, riding next to each other rather than one behind the other. The question it raises is how much of a lane a motorcycle may use, not whether it can move between lanes.

These terms are not interchangeable in law. Each jurisdiction that addresses the practice chooses its own wording and its own definitions. Some U.S. states and some other countries define the terms differently. A word that covers a maneuver in one place may not cover the same maneuver somewhere else.

That distinction matters for anyone reading a statute or a rule book. A law that permits filtering under set conditions does not automatically permit splitting through moving traffic. Utah's statute is written as a filtering law, while California's vehicle code defines splitting.

The words also get blurred in everyday conversation. Riders and drivers often say "lane splitting" to mean any movement between lanes. When the question is what a rider may legally do in a specific place, the term written into that place's rules is the one that counts.

California is the only U.S. state where lane splitting has long been broadly accepted without an explicit prohibition. Assembly Bill 51, signed in 2016, gave the practice a formal definition in the California Vehicle Code.

For years, California's position rested on an absence rather than a grant of permission. The vehicle code did not forbid a motorcyclist from riding between lanes of traffic moving in the same direction. As a result, riders treated the maneuver as an accepted part of traveling on the state's roads.

That arrangement left a gap. A practice can be tolerated without being described anywhere in statute. Before 2016, nothing in the code said what lane splitting actually was.

Assembly Bill 51 closed that gap by putting a definition into the code. With the term defined, the practice gained a recognized place in state law instead of existing only by omission.

The bill did a second thing. It directed the California Highway Patrol to develop educational safety guidelines for lane splitting. Under the law, the agency's role is to inform riders and drivers about safer practice.

Those guidelines are not binding law. They are advice from the state's highway patrol. The legislature did not write them into the vehicle code as enforceable rules. A rider who departs from them is not violating a lane splitting statute for that reason alone.

The law also sets no fixed speed limit for splitting lanes. It does not cap how fast a motorcycle may travel between vehicles. Nor does it cap the speed gap between the motorcycle and the traffic around it. That leaves California without the kind of numeric thresholds that filtering laws in some other states use.

The lack of a lane splitting speed cap does not suspend other traffic rules. Posted speed limits still apply to a motorcyclist moving between lanes, just as they apply to every other vehicle on the road.

For drivers, the practical meaning follows from the same facts. A motorcycle passing between lanes in California is performing a maneuver that state law defines and does not prohibit. A driver who sees one approaching is sharing the road with a recognized practice, not watching a violation.

Utah allows motorcyclists to filter between lanes only when traffic is stopped. The rider must also be on a road with a speed limit of 45 mph or less and travel no faster than 15 mph. The state's lane filtering law took effect in 2019.

A rider has to meet all three conditions at once for the maneuver to fall within the law. Missing any one of them takes the rider outside the permission the statute grants.

The stopped-traffic requirement rules out filtering through moving cars. It describes a line of vehicles that is not moving, such as a queue waiting at a red light. Traffic that is only slow does not meet that condition. Once the queue starts moving again, the condition no longer holds. This separates Utah's rule from lane splitting in the broader sense, where a rider passes between lanes of moving vehicles.

The 45 mph threshold refers to the road's speed limit, not the speed traffic is actually traveling. A rider on a road with a limit of 45 mph or below can filter when traffic there comes to a halt. A road with a higher speed limit falls outside the law, even if the cars on it have stopped. In practice, that leaves out Utah's interstates, whose posted limits sit well above that threshold.

The 15 mph cap applies to the motorcycle itself. It sets the fastest a rider may travel while moving between the stopped vehicles. A rider going faster than that is no longer filtering within the law's terms.

Utah writes its speed figures directly into the statute. Riders there have specific numbers to follow. Each condition can be checked against a fixed number or a plain fact about the traffic. Taken together, the three conditions confine the practice to roads with lower posted limits and to traffic that has come to a standstill.

Arizona, Montana and Colorado each have laws that permit motorcycle lane filtering under conditions written into their own statutes. None of the three gives riders the broad acceptance of riding between lanes that California has long allowed.

The operative term in all three states is filtering, not splitting. Filtering generally describes a rider moving between vehicles that are stopped or barely moving, often while traffic waits at a red light. Lane splitting through traffic moving at normal speed is a broader maneuver. Each of these states wrote its law around the narrower practice.

The conditions in these laws generally address two things: how fast the surrounding traffic may be moving and how fast the rider may travel while passing between vehicles. Some also limit the kinds of roads where the maneuver applies.

Each legislature drafted its own rules. As a result, the conditions are not identical from one state to the next. A rider who knows the filtering rules in one of these states should not assume they apply after crossing into another.

A conditional filtering law sets out when the maneuver is allowed. It does not leave riders to judge for themselves. Outside those conditions, moving between lanes falls back under the ordinary traffic rules that apply to every vehicle.

Together with Utah, these three states form a small group that permits a limited version of the practice by statute. That sets them apart from most U.S. states. In those states, riding between lanes is prohibited outright or ruled out by laws requiring vehicles to stay within a single lane.

Riders can check the current statute text before filtering in Arizona, Montana or Colorado. Guidance from each state's motor vehicle or highway safety agency covers the same ground. For drivers in these states, the laws explain why a motorcycle may legally pass between stopped cars at an intersection.

Most U.S. states ban lane splitting because their traffic codes either prohibit riding between lanes outright or require every vehicle to stay within a single marked lane. A motorcyclist who rides the line between two rows of cars breaks one of those rules, even where no law uses the term lane splitting.

The first route is an explicit prohibition. Some state codes include a motorcycle-specific section that bars riders from operating between lanes of traffic or between adjacent rows of vehicles. That wording leaves little room for interpretation. It covers moving and stopped traffic alike. It rules out splitting at highway speed and also rules out filtering to the front of a line at a red light.

The second route is less direct. Most state traffic codes require a driver on a road divided into marked lanes to keep the vehicle entirely within one lane as nearly as practicable. A driver may leave that lane only after confirming the move can be made safely. A motorcycle counts as a vehicle under these codes. Its rider carries the same duty. Straddling a lane line to pass slower cars does not fit within it.

Many states pair the single-lane rule with a provision that entitles motorcycles to the full use of a lane. The same provision bars other drivers from crowding a motorcycle out of its lane. In effect, it treats each lane as space for one vehicle at a time. A rider squeezing past a car inside that car's lane runs against the same logic.

Some codes go a step further. They prohibit a motorcycle from overtaking and passing in the same lane occupied by the vehicle being passed. That rule covers in-lane passing, which sits between an outright ban on riding between lanes and a general lane-use requirement.

The charge a rider faces depends on which statute a state relies on. In states without an exception, moving between lanes can lead to a citation for an improper lane change, unsafe passing or a lane-use violation.

This structure also explains why allowing the practice in these states takes new legislation. A state that wants to permit filtering cannot just stop enforcing its existing rules. Lawmakers have to write an exception into the traffic code. That exception typically spells out where, when and at what speed riders may move between vehicles.

Motorcycle filtering is permitted in the U.K., and the Highway Code addresses it directly. The guidance covers riders moving through slow-moving traffic, not riding at speed between lanes of free-flowing vehicles.

The Highway Code is the official rulebook for road users in Great Britain, meaning England, Scotland and Wales. Northern Ireland publishes its own version. The code covers drivers, motorcyclists, cyclists and pedestrians in one document. The same text that speaks to riders about filtering is also the text drivers are expected to know.

British guidance uses the word "filtering" for this maneuver. It refers to passing between vehicles that are stopped or crawling, such as a queue of cars waiting at a red light or held up in congestion. Reading the code as permission to ride between lanes of traffic at highway speed would misread it.

The section written for motorcyclists places filtering inside its advice on maneuvering. Riders are told to know what is behind them and to their sides before they move. In traffic queues, the code tells them to watch for pedestrians crossing between vehicles. It also flags vehicles coming out of junctions or changing lanes. When filtering in slow-moving traffic, riders are told to take care and keep their speed low.

The code sets no number for that speed. It leaves the rider to judge what "low" means against the traffic around them.

Drivers get their own reference to the practice. The section on other road users warns that motorcyclists can be hard to see. It names filtering through traffic among the moments a rider is difficult to spot, along with overtaking and approaching from behind.

The code contains two kinds of instruction. Rules written with "must" or "must not" reflect legal requirements backed by legislation. Rules written with "should" or "should not" are advisory. The filtering advice uses the advisory wording. The code states that failing to follow advisory rules can still be used as evidence in court proceedings to help establish liability.

France introduced a legal lane filtering experiment in 2021 that applied in certain departments, not across the whole country. Riding between lanes of traffic is a widespread practice among motorcyclists in France. The experiment gave that practice a legal framework in the areas it covered.

Departments are administrative divisions of France. The 2021 experiment was limited to certain of them. As a result, a rider's legal position depended on where in France the riding took place.

A maneuver covered by the experiment in a participating department had no such coverage in a department outside the program. Location, not the maneuver itself, determined how the rules applied.

The word "experiment" matters here. It describes a trial arrangement tied to defined areas. It is not a single permanent rule written for the whole country. An experimental framework can be extended, changed or ended. The rules in force for a department at the time of riding are the ones that apply.

Across the rest of Europe, the picture is not uniform. Each country sets its own traffic law. Approaches to riding between lanes differ from one national code to the next. Some countries address the practice directly in their road rules. Others leave it to general provisions on lane use and overtaking.

That variation has a practical consequence for riders who cross borders. A maneuver accepted in one country may be treated differently a short drive away. The national traffic code of the country a rider is in determines what applies.

Motorcycle lane filtering rules in Australia depend on the state or territory where a rider is on the road. New South Wales permits filtering, but only under set conditions.

Each Australian state and territory sets and enforces its own road rules. A maneuver that is lawful in one state may not be lawful across the border. A rider's options in heavy traffic can change partway through a long interstate trip.

Permission to filter does not travel with the motorcycle or the license. A rider licensed in one state must follow the road rules of the state where they are riding at the time.

In New South Wales, filtering is a conditional maneuver rather than a general right to move between lanes at will. The conditions govern where, when and how a rider may move past slower or stopped vehicles. A rider who meets those conditions is filtering legally. A rider who moves between vehicles outside them is not covered by the permission.

That structure places NSW among the jurisdictions that allow filtering within written limits, rather than leaving the practice broadly open. The maneuver is permitted, but the state defines its boundaries.

Each state and territory publishes its current rules through its own road authority. Riders who plan to filter in Australia need to check the rules for the specific state or territory they are in before relying on them.

The main safety factor in lane splitting is the speed difference between the motorcycle and the traffic beside it. A small difference gives the rider and nearby drivers time to react. A large one cuts that time down.

That speed difference matters more than the rider's absolute speed. A motorcycle passing cars that are crawling has a very different margin than one passing cars moving close to the posted limit. When surrounding traffic speeds up, the gaps between vehicles tend to close. Drivers also start changing lanes more often. For riders, that shift is usually the point to merge back into a full lane.

Driver awareness is the second factor. Many drivers do not expect a vehicle to appear between lanes, especially in places where the practice is rare or banned. A motorcycle has a narrow profile. It can sit inside a car's blind spot or disappear behind a roof pillar or headrest. A rider between lanes has no confirmation of being seen until a driver's movement shows it.

Visibility affects that awareness. Bright riding gear and a consistent position between lanes make a motorcycle easier to pick out in traffic. A rider who weaves or changes position often is harder for drivers to track.

Mirror checks work in both directions. Drivers in slow or stopped traffic can glance at their side and rearview mirrors before drifting toward a lane line. A turn signal gives a rider coming from behind clear warning that a gap is about to close. Riders, for their part, watch the front wheels of nearby cars. A wheel turning toward the lane line often signals a lane change before the body of the car moves.

Mirrors are also a physical obstacle. Trucks, buses and wide pickups leave narrower gaps. Their mirrors can sit at a height that lines up with a motorcycle's handlebars. Saddlebags and wide touring motorcycles add width of their own. Passing between two large vehicles leaves very little room for error.

Stopped traffic brings separate hazards. A passenger may open a door into the gap, or a pedestrian may step out between cars. Checking a side mirror before opening a door reduces one of the risks specific to a traffic jam. Drivers can also hold a steady position in their lane rather than moving over to block a motorcycle.

None of these habits makes lane splitting legal where it is prohibited. Local law still decides whether the maneuver is allowed at all.

American Airlines Cancels 8 International Routes: See Full List Now

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

American Airlines is making significant changes to its international network, with eight routes that operated during the first half of this year now absent from its corresponding 2027 schedule. The affected services range from short flights to Canada and Mexico to intercontinental connections linking the United States with Europe, the Middle East, and Australia.

However, the withdrawals are not all attributable to weak demand. An analysis of US Department of Transportation (DOT) data reveals that some routes were filling more than 80% of their available seats, while others struggled to reach 60%. The changes also highlight American's distinctive international strategy, which is considerably more focused on Latin America and the Caribbean than its rivals.

The eight affected routes include departures from seven of the airline's US hubs, with Phoenix Sky Harbor International Airport (PHX) accounting for two cancellations. The changes span different circumstances, including transferring the daily service from Philadelphia International Airport (PHL) to Doha Hamad International Airport (DOH) to its partner, Qatar Airways, the withdrawal of seasonal transatlantic services, and the discontinuation of regional flights. Some services ended earlier in 2026, while Phoenix–Monterrey is scheduled to finish this month.

The long-haul withdrawals are particularly notable. American introduced a new route from Dallas to Zurich Airport (ZRH) this past summer, and that won't be returning next year. Neither will the limited seasonal service from Los Angeles International Airport (LAX) to Brisbane Airport (BNE), which had operated alongside its joint-business partner Qantas. Also missing from the 2027 schedule is the long-time route from Miami to Paris Charles De Gaulle Airport (CDG), which the airline first launched in 1992, and then downgraded to a seasonal service post-pandemic. The planned December 2026 resumption has been canceled.

Shorter international routes have also been affected. American ended JFK–Toronto in May, choosing to consolidate its service to Canada's largest city from LaGuardia Airport (LGA). Phoenix lost its Tijuana connection in August, with Monterrey scheduled to follow in November. Meanwhile, the route from Chicago O'Hare International Airport (ORD) to San José was suspended in April, and while local reports at the time described the interruption as temporary, it is not reappearing on the 2027 schedule.

Passenger demand provides one explanation for the cuts, but not the whole story. The two Phoenix routes recorded particularly weak load factors during the first half of 2026, with Tijuana International Airport (TIJ) falling as low as 40% at the start of the year, and Monterrey's General Mariano Escobedo International Airport (MTY) not performing much better. Cutting those routes seems like an obvious decision.

By contrast, Philadelphia–Doha achieved 85.8%, but its suspension occurred due to Middle East hostilities, and oneworld partner Qatar Airways subsequently restored nonstop connectivity. Other withdrawals reflect seasonality and aircraft re-allocation, but hanging over all of this are higher fuel costs. Reuters reports that this has prompted American to reconsider less-profitable flying, with the airline saying:

“As part of targeted actions to strengthen the airline, American will moderate its planned growth.”

The carrier will cease serving this corridor this winter.

American's international operations are substantially different to arch-rivals Delta Air Lines and United Airlines. Partly due to having a smaller widebody fleet (especially after heavy pandemic-era retirements), and partly due to its hub placement and legacy operations, three out of every four American Airlines international flights heads south, rather than across the Atlantic or Pacific.

This geographical concentration distinguishes American from its two largest US competitors. An analysis of 1H 2026 DOT data indicates that approximately 75% of American's international departures serve Latin America and the Caribbean, with the carrier now offering more than 100 destinations across the region. This compares with less than half of all international flights for Delta and United. By contrast, Delta has a substantially greater European, Middle Eastern and African presence, while United operates a considerably larger proportion of its international flights to Asia-Pacific.

It's not surprising, then, that American's international capacity is heavily concentrated at Miami International Airport (MIA) and Dallas/Fort Worth International Airport (DFW). In the first half of 2027, Miami will handle 39% of the airline's international departures across its ten hub airports, while Dallas will be responsible for a further 24%. Combined, the two airports account for approximately two-thirds of all international traffic for the carrier.

However, there is a slight shift coming. While Miami is currently scheduled to grow a further 4.9% with the 2027 schedule, it is Philadelphia and New York (JFK) that are both expected to grow approximately 7% next year as American increases its transatlantic capacity, notably with the addition of new Airbus A321XLR routes. So these eight route cancellations ultimately represent a redistribution of capacity rather than a retreat from international flying.
